        (full procedural low-poly city — scene composition)

================================================================================
END OF PART 40 — v1.17 / v8.9 ADDITIONS: BETTER MODELS THROUGH BETTER GENERATION
A production-grade generation pipeline: better prompts + multi-stage
workflow + mesh quality + structured TS + PBR materials + parametric
techniques + validation + scene composition. Adds E19 PBR + UV
completeness, E20 hard-constraint compliance (thin spike detector),
and E21 target triangle budget to the existing 18 non-blocking
validators. Re-exports `pbrMaterial`, `seededRandom`, and
`triCountSummary` on `window.lblSpec.helpers` alongside the PART 35
tris bundle and the PART 39 curvedPart / validateModel / snapshotToPng
bundle. Style-agnostic, applies to every .ts factory, every JSON/JS
blueprint, every subject. No new dependency, no new export path, no
new mandatory field, no removed / weakened rule.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 41 — v1.18 / v8.10 ADDITIONS: HIGH-DETAIL GENERATION
================================================================================
Source: the maintainer's "System High-Detail Update Plan" (2026-08).
The plan targets a specific gap: current models ship in the ~20 KB
range, while higher-end systems (img2threejs and similar) ship in
the >60 KB range. The gap is not about geometry size — it's about
GEOMETRIC DENSITY, the count of secondary and tertiary forms, and
the discipline of authoring detail BEFORE writing code. PART 41
adds the rules, validators, and helpers that close this gap.

PART 41 layers on top of — never replaces — PART 1, PART 2, PART 3,
PART 4, PART 17, PART 27, PART 30, PART 31, PART 32, PART 34, PART 35,
PART 36, PART 37, PART 38, PART 38.22, PART 39, PART 40, PART 64,
PART 65, and PART 66. PART 41 reinforces PART 40's production-grade
pipeline framing with a stronger density-first discipline and a
5-stage pipeline (PART 40's 4 stages become the first 4 of PART 41's
5, with Stage 4 "Surface Detail" promoted from a Stage 2 sub-bullet
to its own stage).

  41.0  CENTRAL PRINCIPLE
  -----------------------

    ONE rule drives the rest of PART 41:

      PREFER GEOMETRIC DENSITY OVER CODE BREVITY.

    The current models stay low-detail because of three
    anti-patterns the AI has fallen into:

      1. Single-pass generation — the AI tries to ship
         a complete factory in one shot, which caps the
         component count at "what fits in one mental
         pass". PART 41 splits the work into 5 STAGES
         (41.2) so each stage can focus on one concern.
      2. Overly DRY / compact code style — the AI tries
         to minimize line count, which minimizes
         secondary and tertiary forms. PART 41 inverts
         this: when in doubt, add a layer. The
         `prefer many small forms over few large ones`
         rule (41.3.3) is the explicit instruction.
      3. No detail inventory before code — the AI
         jumps into geometry without listing what
         identity / secondary / tertiary details the
         subject MUST have. PART 41 requires a
         DETAIL INVENTORY (41.1) before any geometry
         is written, and the inventory becomes the
         factory's checklist.

    The 7 PROMPT RULES (41.8) capture the same idea as
    7 sentences the AI can paste into its own system
    prompt. They are the "I will" half of the contract;
    41.1-41.7 are the "I will" half spelled out in
    detail.

    PART 41 is style-agnostic. The density rules
    apply to low-poly, mid-poly, high-poly,
    smooth-shaded, stylized, photoreal, voxel,
    hand-painted, toon / cel, retro-PSX, hologram,
    wireframe, and any combination. A dense low-poly
    model is still low-poly (it's the per-form triangle
    count that stays low); a dense photoreal model is
    still photoreal (it just has more PBR detail
    layers). PART 41's density rules do not change
    the style axis from PART 38.22; they add a
    DENSITY axis on top of it.

  41.1  DETAIL INVENTORY (before writing any geometry)
  ---------------------------------------------------

    Before the AI writes a single line of geometry, it
    MUST produce a DETAIL INVENTORY. The inventory is a
    structured list of what the model needs to ship with,
    broken into 4 categories.

      1. Identity-defining details (what makes it a
         knight, an AKM, a teapot, a tree).
      2. Secondary forms (the layers, plates, and
         props that frame the identity).
      3. Tertiary micro-details (rivets, seams,
         damage, folds).
      4. Material zones (which surfaces get which
         material family).

    The inventory is published on the model's metadata
    so the renderer can read it and the AI can audit
    it.

    On the TS path, the factory publishes the inventory
    on `userData.meta.detailInventory`:

      meta.detailInventory = {
        identity: [
          'plasma canister with glowing rune',
          'two-pronged claw grip'
        ],
        secondary: [
          'left pauldron with 3 angled plates',
          'right pauldron with matching 3 plates',
          'chest plate with central power core inset',
          'back-mounted exhaust vents (4)'
        ],
        tertiary: [
          'rivet rows on every pauldron plate (8 per row)',
          'seam lines between chest and abdomen plate',
          'battle damage chips on left pauldron (3)',
          'tattered cape hem with 7 micro-tears'
        ],
        materialZones: [
          'plate (dark steel) for all armor plates',
          'plateDark (burnished) for inset channels',
          'plateCold (blue-tinted) for the power core',
          'cloth (faded crimson) for the cape',
          'leather (dark brown) for the belt and straps',
          'emissive (cyan, intensity 1.4) for the rune'
        ]
      }

    On the JSON/JS path, the blueprint publishes the
    same shape on `meta.detailInventory`. The
    renderer's E22 validator (41.9) checks the actual
    mesh count against the inventory's secondary +
    tertiary totals.

    The inventory is OPTIONAL. When omitted, the
    renderer's E22 check is a no-op; the E23 minimum
    mesh count check (41.9) still applies if
    `meta.minMeshes` is declared.

  41.2  THE 5-STAGE PIPELINE
  -------------------------

    PART 41 promotes PART 40's 4-stage pipeline to a
    5-stage pipeline, with one explicit change:
    PART 40's Stage 2 (Geometry refinement) becomes
    PART 41's Stages 2 + 3 (Structure + Form Density),
    and a new Stage 4 (Surface Detail) is added
    between Form Density and Materials. The 5 stages
    are:

      Stage 1 — Blockout (silhouette + proportions)
      Stage 2 — Structure (major plates, primary
                separations, large secondary forms)
      Stage 3 — Form Density (layered plates,
                overlapping pieces, bevels, thickness)
      Stage 4 — Surface Detail (rivets, engravings,
                straps, seams, battle damage as
                geometry; cloth folds; micro-chips)
      Stage 5 — Materials & Polish (multiple material
                variants per part, roughness /
                metalness variation, final hierarchy
                cleanup)

    Each stage is INDEPENDENT — the AI can re-run a
    single stage without redoing the others, and each
    stage ADDS to the previous stage's output. A
    mistake in Stage 4 (forgot to add rivets) does NOT
    require redoing Stage 2.

  41.2.1  Stage 1 — Blockout.

    Goal: lock in the SILHOUETTE and the PROPORTIONS
    using the lowest possible component count (often
    < 200 tris and < 10 meshes for the whole model).
    The blockout may look crude — it doesn't need
    textures, materials, secondary forms, or detail;
    it needs to be RECOGNIZABLE at 256x256
    black-on-white (PART 39.4.1 silhouette test) and
    to pass the PART 41.5 silhouette & proportion
    pass.

    This is the same as PART 40.2.1, with one
    tightening: the blockout's component count is now
    an UPPER BOUND for Stages 2-5, not a target.
    A blockout that ships 30 meshes will not
    magically grow to 300 meshes in Stage 3; the
    blockout is the seed, and Stages 2-5 add to it.

  41.2.2  Stage 2 — Structure.

    Goal: add the MAJOR plates, primary separations,
    and large secondary forms. This is the stage
    where the model's secondary forms (41.1) start
    to appear. For a character, this is the stage
    where the chest plate, pauldrons, and back
    vents are placed. For a prop, this is where
    the major sub-assemblies (barrel + receiver +
    stock) are placed.

    Output target: 30-50% of the model's final mesh
    count. If the final target is 250 meshes (41.3.1),
    Stage 2 should ship 75-125 meshes.

  41.2.3  Stage 3 — Form Density.

    Goal: add LAYERED plates, OVERLAPPING pieces,
    BEVELS, and THICKNESS. This is the stage where
    flat boxes become armor (overlapping thinner
    boxes), where flat surfaces become faceted
    (bevels), and where single pieces become
    stacks (thickness via a thin box on top of a
    box). The output target is 50-70% of the
    final mesh count.

    The `prefer many small forms over few large
    ones` rule (41.3.3) starts to dominate here.

  41.2.4  Stage 4 — Surface Detail.

    Goal: add the TERTIARY micro-details. Rivets,
    engravings, straps, seams, battle damage, cloth
    folds, tattered edges, micro-chips — all as
    REAL GEOMETRY, not just color. The output
    target is 80-100% of the final mesh count.
    This is the stage where the model earns the
    "denser than 50 KB" tag.

    The `use InstancedMesh for repeated detail`
    rule (PART 39.3.4 / 40.6.4) is the canonical
    way to ship the rivet rows, plate layers, and
    damage chips without blowing the mesh count
    past the renderer's E18 500-mesh sanity check.

  41.2.5  Stage 5 — Materials & Polish.

    Goal: apply a full PBR texture set (PART 40.5)
    with style-specific texturing (PART 40.5.2)
    AND the material variants from 41.6. The
    output target is 100% of the final mesh count
    + the material zones are mapped correctly.
    The hierarchy is final; the runtime is
    complete (`userData.sculptRuntime` is set,
    every named node has a part library entry,
    every part library entry has a socket where
    applicable — see 41.7).

  41.3  FORCE HIGHER COMPONENT COUNT
  ---------------------------------

    PART 41 adds hard rules in the system prompt
    that push the AI toward denser models. These
    are NOT style-dependent — a 200-mesh low-poly
    character is denser than a 50-mesh mid-poly
    character, and the rules apply to both.

  41.3.1  Minimum mesh count targets.

    PART 41 declares the following floor targets.
    The AI MUST meet or exceed them; the renderer's
    E23 minimum-mesh-count validator (41.9)
    surfaces a non-blocking warning when a model
    falls below.

      - Character / mascot / creature / humanoid:
        200 meshes minimum (target 250-400).
      - Vehicle (car, truck, tank, ship, plane):
        150 meshes minimum (target 200-350).
      - Weapon (rifle, pistol, sword, bow):
        80 meshes minimum (target 100-200).
      - Prop (crate, barrel, furniture, tool):
        40 meshes minimum (target 60-150).
      - Architecture (building, wall, bridge, tower):
        100 meshes minimum (target 150-300).
      - Scene (forest, village, city, planet):
        300 meshes minimum for the base
        (the scene's individual objects follow
        their own floor; InstancedMesh counts as 1
        mesh from the perspective of this rule).

    When a model declares `meta.subjectClass` from
    PART 38, the E23 check uses that class. When
    omitted, E23 falls back to `meta.subject` string
    matching (the legacy heuristic).

  41.3.2  Required repeated micro-elements.

    The AI MUST include at least 3 of the following
    repeated micro-element patterns in every
    character / vehicle / architecture model:

      - Rivet rows on every armor plate (8+ per row).
      - Plate layers with overlapping thinner boxes.
      - Damage chips on at least one major surface
        (3+ chips for characters, 5+ for vehicles).
      - Seam lines between every two adjacent plates.
      - Strap loops on every back-mounted prop.
      - Bolt heads on every major fastener (4+ per
        joint).
      - Panel gap shadow boxes (thin black boxes
        behind every panel seam).

    For low-density styles (voxel, hand-painted),
    the AI substitutes blockier equivalents (chamfer
    edges, painted highlights) — the pattern is
    preserved, the per-element complexity drops.

  41.3.3  Prefer many small forms over few large
          ones.

    When the AI is about to write a factory call
    for a `BoxGeometry(2, 1, 1)`, the AI should
    pause and consider: would 4 smaller boxes
    (`BoxGeometry(1, 1, 1)` + `BoxGeometry(1, 0.5,
    1)` + `BoxGeometry(0.5, 0.5, 1)` + `BoxGeometry(1,
    0.25, 1)`) stacked with slight offsets produce
    a more readable form? If yes, the AI writes the
    4-box factory, not the 1-box factory.

    The tradeoff: more lines of code, more mesh
    count, but a denser, more readable model. The
    `prefer geometric density over code brevity`
    central principle (41.0) is the explicit
    instruction. Code brevity is a soft preference
    that yields to density.

  41.3.4  Explicitly ask for "secondary" and
          "tertiary" detail layers.

    The AI's authoring-time checklist (PART 39.6)
    gets a new line: "does this model have a
    secondary detail layer? A tertiary one?" If
    the answer to either is no, the AI adds the
    layer before shipping.

  41.4  STRONGER GEOMETRY VOCABULARY
  ----------------------------------

    PART 41 teaches the system to freely use the
    following 6 patterns. The patterns are the
    vocabulary of density — every pattern ships
    with a code snippet and a use-case list.

  41.4.1  Overlapping boxes for layered armor.

    A single `BoxGeometry(1.0, 0.5, 0.8)` chest
    plate becomes 5 overlapping thinner boxes:

      - Main plate: `BoxGeometry(1.0, 0.40, 0.75)`.
      - Top inset: `BoxGeometry(0.9, 0.05, 0.70)`,
        offset Y +0.20.
      - Bottom inset: `BoxGeometry(0.9, 0.05, 0.70)`,
        offset Y -0.20.
      - Left bevel: `BoxGeometry(0.05, 0.45, 0.78)`,
        offset X -0.50.
      - Right bevel: `BoxGeometry(0.05, 0.45, 0.78)`,
        offset X +0.50.

    The 5 boxes share the same material (the
    `plate` material variant from 41.6.1) and
    are merged into a single mesh for draw-call
    efficiency. The visual effect: the chest
    plate reads as LAYERED, not flat.

  41.4.2  Multiple thin cylinders for rings /
          straps.

    A single thick `CylinderGeometry(0.10, 0.10,
    0.50, 16)` belt buckle ring becomes 3 thin
    rings:

      - Outer ring: `CylinderGeometry(0.10, 0.10,
        0.02, 16)`.
      - Inner ring: `CylinderGeometry(0.08, 0.08,
        0.025, 16)`, offset Y +0.001.
      - Decorative ring: `CylinderGeometry(0.06,
        0.06, 0.03, 16)`, offset Y +0.002 with
        a different material variant.

    The 3 rings share the same XZ center but
    have different Y heights; they read as a
    STACK, not a single solid ring. The
    `materialVariants(family, opts)` helper
    (41.10) is the canonical way to assign the
    right material to each ring.

  41.4.3  Small spheres for rivets / bolts.

    A `SphereGeometry(0.01, 8, 6)` rivet becomes
    the canonical unit of tertiary detail. The AI
    places it via `InstancedMesh` (PART 39.3.4) at
    the corner of every armor plate, every strap
    junction, and every bolt hole. A single
    `InstancedMesh` of 200 rivet instances
    contributes 1 mesh to the E18 500-mesh sanity
    check and 200 instances of tertiary detail to
    the model's density score.

  41.4.4  Thin boxes for seams, engravings,
          scratches.

    A `BoxGeometry(0.05, 0.005, 0.30)` seam line
    is the canonical secondary detail. The AI
    places it along every plate boundary, every
    panel joint, and every visible scratch. The
    seam is 0.005 units thick (just thick enough
    to render without z-fighting) and uses a
    `plateDark` material variant (41.6.1) so it
    reads as a SHADOW LINE, not a panel.

  41.4.5  Slightly rotated / offset pieces for
          organic feel.

    A perfectly axis-aligned `BoxGeometry(1, 1, 1)`
    is a robotic feel. A `BoxGeometry(1, 1, 1)`
    with `rotation.y = 0.02` and `position.y = 0.005`
    is an ORGANIC feel. The AI applies tiny
    rotations and offsets to every secondary
    form to break the grid. The rotation is
    deterministic (seededRandom from PART 40.6.2
    / 41.10) so the model is reproducible.

  41.4.6  Multi-layer cloth (cape, cloak, banner).

    A single `PlaneGeometry(1, 1)` cape is a flag.
    A cape made of 3 layered `PlaneGeometry` with
    slight X offsets and shared Y folds is a
    CAPE. The 3 layers are:

      - Outer cape: full-size, with a `cloth`
        material variant (41.6.2).
      - Middle cape: 0.95 scale, 0.05 X offset,
        with a `clothDark` material variant.
      - Inner cape: 0.90 scale, -0.05 X offset,
        with a `cloth` material variant (matches
        the outer for symmetry).

    The 3 layers are placed on the SAME vertex
    group but with different transform matrices,
    so the cape can be animated (Part 30's
    bone-based animation can swing all 3 layers
    together while the inner one lags slightly).

  41.5  SILHOUETTE & PROPORTION PASS
  ----------------------------------

    PART 41 promotes the PART 39.4 silhouette
    test from a heuristic to a FIRST-CLASS PASS
    with explicit checks. The pass runs AFTER
    Stage 1 (blockout) and AFTER Stage 2
    (structure), before any tertiary detail is
    added — a model with broken proportions at
    Stage 1 will still have broken proportions
    at Stage 5 if the pass doesn't catch it.

  41.5.1  Multi-angle readability.

    The AI renders the blockout at 6 canonical
    angles (front, 3/4 front, side, 3/4 back,
    back, top) at 256x256 black-on-white. A
    model that reads as "knight" from front
    but as "blob" from top is a Stage 1 FAIL
    that needs proportion adjustment before
    Stage 2 can start.

    The renderer's PART 39.4.1 silhouette test
    fires on the front view; PART 41 adds the
    5 additional angles as authoring-time
    requirements (the renderer's runtime
    surface does not enforce them — the AI
    enforces them at authoring time).

  41.5.2  Limb thickness and joint hierarchy.

    For character subjects, the AI checks the
    PART 38.16 bounding-box proportions
    (E16 validator) at the blockout stage, not
    just at the final stage. A leg that is
    60% of the torso's height at the blockout
    stage is correct; a leg that is 60% of
    the torso's height at the final stage is
    also correct, but the AI would have
    shipped 300 meshes of the wrong model.
    Catching it at Stage 1 saves work.

  41.5.3  Weight distribution and grounded stance.

    The AI checks the model's center of mass at
    the blockout stage. A character whose center
    of mass is 0.05 units off the XZ origin
    needs a stance adjustment; the AI adds a
    subtle counterweight (a hip pack, an offset
    boot, a shifted pauldron) to bring the
    center of mass back to the origin. The
    adjustment is part of the blockout; the
    detail inventory (41.1) gets a new entry
    for it.

  41.5.4  Prop scale relative to body.

    For character-with-prop models (a knight
    holding a sword, a mage holding a staff),
    the AI checks the prop's scale against the
    body at the blockout stage. A sword that
    is as tall as the knight is a Stage 1
    FAIL; the AI resizes the prop before Stage 2
    can start. The PART 38.16 E16 validator's
    `expectedProportions` field is the
    authoring-time surface for this check.

  41.6  MATERIAL SYSTEM UPGRADE
  ----------------------------

    PART 41 expands the PART 40.5 material
    system with VARIANTS PER FAMILY. The base
    family is a single material; the variants
    are tuned copies that read as different
    surface treatments.

  41.6.1  Material family variants.

    A "plate" material family has at least 4
    variants:

      - `plate` — base dark steel.
        (color: 0x4a4a4a, roughness: 0.7,
         metalness: 0.8)
      - `plateDark` — burnished, slightly darker.
        (color: 0x2a2a2a, roughness: 0.5,
         metalness: 0.9)
      - `plateLight` — polished, slightly lighter.
        (color: 0x6a6a6a, roughness: 0.3,
         metalness: 0.9)
      - `plateCold` — blue-tinted, weathered.
        (color: 0x4a5560, roughness: 0.8,
         metalness: 0.6)

    The `materialVariants(family, opts)` helper
    (41.10) returns a configured
    MeshStandardMaterial for each variant in the
    family, so the AI can call
    `M.plate`, `M.plateDark`, `M.plateLight`,
    `M.plateCold` without re-deriving the
    parameters.

    The 4 default families are:

      - `plate` (armor, vehicle hull, weapon
        receiver) — 4 variants.
      - `cloth` (cape, cloak, banner, tabard) —
        4 variants: `cloth`, `clothDark`,
        `clothLight`, `clothFaded`.
      - `leather` (belt, strap, glove, boot) —
        4 variants: `leather`, `leatherDark`,
        `leatherLight`, `leatherWorn`.
      - `stone` (architecture, ruin, statue) —
        4 variants: `stone`, `stoneDark`,
        `stoneLight`, `stoneWeathered`.

    Custom families (e.g. `energy`, `organic`,
    `crystal`) are added by passing a custom
    variant list to `materialVariants`.

  41.6.2  Dedicated scratch / damage / engrave
          materials.

    Three named material slots are reserved for
    the model's tertiary detail:

      - `M.scratch` — thin lines along armor
        plates (color: 0x888888, roughness:
        0.4, metalness: 0.7). Applied to the
        thin boxes from 41.4.4.
      - `M.damage` — chipped edges on damaged
        plates (color: 0x2a1a1a, roughness:
        0.9, metalness: 0.2). Applied to the
        damage chips from 41.4.3.
      - `M.engrave` — recessed rune / symbol /
        panel-line detail (color: 0x1a1a1a,
        roughness: 0.6, metalness: 0.3).
        Applied to the engravings from 41.4.4.

    These three materials are the SECONDARY
    palette of the model; the four family
    palettes (41.6.1) are the PRIMARY. A
    model that uses only the primary palette
    is a Stage 3 model; a model that also
    uses the secondary palette is a Stage 4
    model.

  41.6.3  Clear metal vs cloth vs leather vs
          stone separation.

    A model that uses `plate` for a leather
    strap or `cloth` for a metal pauldron is
    a Stage 2 FAIL. The AI's authoring-time
    audit (PART 39.6's checklist) gets a
    new line: "does every surface use the
    RIGHT family?" The renderer's runtime
    surface does not enforce this — it's an
    authoring-time check, like 41.5.3.

  41.6.4  Optional simple procedural variation.

    The AI can add procedural variation to
    any of the family variants by passing
    an `opts.noise` parameter to
    `materialVariants`. The helper applies
    a deterministic Perlin-noise color jitter
    (using the seededRandom from PART 40.6.2
    / 41.10) and returns a `MeshStandardMaterial`
    with the color baked into a `map` (a
    procedurally generated `CanvasTexture`).
    The effect: every instance of `M.plate`
    is slightly different, breaking the
    "robotic feel" without breaking
    reproducibility.

  41.7  HIERARCHY & RUNTIME QUALITY
  ---------------------------------

    PART 41 reinforces PART 40.4.3's
    4-child hierarchy (parts / bones / sockets
    / meta) with two tighter rules.

  41.7.1  Named nodes for every major part.

    Every part in the factory's Group MUST
    have a `.name` property. The
    `lblSpec.helpers.validateModel` walker
    (PART 39.1.2) and the renderer's E17
    bbox-overlap gap detector use the names
    in their warning messages; a part
    without a name produces a "node_7" warning
    that the user cannot act on.

    The AI enforces the rule by always passing
    a `name` argument to the part-library
    factory:

      const leftPauldron = createBox({
        name: 'leftPauldron',
        size: [1.0, 0.3, 0.8],
        ...
      });

    The convention: camelCase, no spaces, no
    underscores, matches the part-library
    primitive name where possible.

  41.7.2  Sockets for weapons / attachments.

    Every character / vehicle model MUST
    expose at least 2 of the 4 canonical
    Socket_* attachment points (PART 33.1[7]):

      - `Socket_Weapon_Primary` — right hand
        or main weapon mount.
      - `Socket_Weapon_Secondary` — left hand
        or off-hand weapon mount.
      - `Socket_Back` — back-mounted prop
        (cape, quiver, backpack).
      - `Socket_Head` — head-mounted prop
        (helmet plume, crown, halo).

    A character with no sockets cannot accept
    the renderer's PART 33.1[7] kitbash
    attachments. The AI's authoring-time
    audit (PART 39.6) gets a new line:
    "does this model expose the canonical
    sockets?"

  41.7.3  Clean parent-child structure.

    The factory's Group tree MUST be
    well-formed: every Mesh has a parent
    Group, every Group has a parent Group or
    is the root, no floating Meshes. The
    renderer's E17 bbox-overlap gap detector
    (PART 39.7.1) already surfaces floating
    parts; PART 41 reinforces the rule with
    an authoring-time audit that runs BEFORE
    Stage 5 (Materials & Polish) so a
    broken tree doesn't get textured.

  41.7.4  Complete `userData.sculptRuntime`.

    The factory MUST publish a complete
    `userData.sculptRuntime` object before
    returning the Group. The complete shape:

      userData.sculptRuntime = {
        // PART 5.6 contract
        nodes: {
          'leftPauldron': leftPauldronMesh,
          'rightPauldron': rightPauldronMesh,
          'chestPlate': chestPlateMesh,
          // ... every named part from 41.7.1
        },
        // PART 33.1[7] sockets
        sockets: {
          'Socket_Weapon_Primary': weaponPrimarySocket,
          'Socket_Weapon_Secondary': weaponSecondarySocket,
          'Socket_Back': backSocket,
          'Socket_Head': headSocket,
        },
        // PART 41.1 detail inventory
        detailInventory: meta.detailInventory,
        // PART 41.3 component count target
        minMeshes: meta.minMeshes,
        // PART 41.2 stage progress
        pipelineStage: 5,  // last completed stage
        // PART 40.2.4 export readiness
        exportReady: true
      }

    A factory that returns the Group without
    populating `userData.sculptRuntime` is
    a Stage 5 FAIL — the renderer's runtime
    cannot attach animations, sockets, or
    inspector UI to a model that hasn't
    published its hooks.

  41.8  THE 7 PROMPT RULES
  -----------------------

    The maintainer's update plan distills 7
    rules the AI can paste into its own
    system prompt. They are the "I will" half
    of the PART 41 contract.

      1. "Prefer geometric density over code
         brevity."
      2. "Every major surface must have at
         least one layer of secondary detail."
      3. "Battle damage, rivets, seams and
         folds must be real geometry, not
         just color."
      4. "Target minimum 200-400 meshes for
         a full character." (And the
         subject-class-specific floors from
         41.3.1.)
      5. "Build in clear passes: Blockout →
         Structure → Density → Surface →
         Polish." (The 5 stages from 41.2.)
      6. "Never leave large flat surfaces
         unbroken." (The seam-line rule
         from 41.4.4 + the bevel rule from
         41.4.1.)
      7. "Use overlapping plates and
         thickness to create readable form."
         (The vocabulary from 41.4.)

    These 7 rules are the AUTHOR-TIME
    checklist. The renderer's E22 + E23
    validators (41.9) are the RUN-TIME
    check; the AI's authoring loop is the
    MID-TIME check.

  41.9  RENDERER-SIDE RUNTIME CONTRACT
  ------------------------------------

    PART 41 adds 2 new non-blocking validators
    to the existing 21:

      - E22 — Detail inventory compliance
        (PART 41.1 / 41.9). If the model
        declares `meta.detailInventory`, the
        actual mesh count should meet or
        exceed the sum of (identity.length +
        secondary.length + tertiary.length).
        The renderer surfaces the actual vs
        declared gap in the warning.
        Models without `meta.detailInventory`
        are exempt.

      - E23 — Minimum mesh count target
        (PART 41.3.1 / 41.9). If the model
        declares `meta.minMeshes`, the actual
        mesh count should meet or exceed the
        target. When `meta.subjectClass` is
        also declared, the renderer falls
        back to the PART 41.3.1 floor for
        that class when `meta.minMeshes` is
        missing. Non-blocking; the model
        still loads.

    All 2 are non-blocking; the model still
    loads, the user sees a toast + a red
    spec chip. They mirror the E1-E21
    pattern and integrate into the existing
    `validate()` / `applyToUI()` / `revalidate()`
    pipeline without any caller-side
    changes.

    What PART 41 does NOT change in the
    renderer runtime:

      - No change to E1-E21 (they stay
        exactly as they were in v1.17 / v8.9).
      - No change to the GLB / OBJ / STL /
        FBX / DAE / USDZ / .ts export
        pipeline.
      - No change to the import map.
      - No change to the loader.
      - No change to the file-picker UI.
      - No change to the scene tree or
        the Rig / Tris / Sockets buttons.
      - No new mandatory field on any
        existing userData namespace — the
        new fields from 41.1
        (`detailInventory`) and 41.3.1
        (`minMeshes`) are OPTIONAL.
      - No new dependency.

  41.10 CANONICAL HELPERS
  -----------------------

    PART 41 adds 2 new canonical helpers to
    the existing `__threeTriHelpers` bundle
    (PART 35 + PART 39 + PART 40). The .ts
    factory can call any of them via
    `window.lblSpec.helpers.<name>` without
    copying the function body.

    - `materialVariants(family, opts)`
        PART 41.6.1 / 41.6.2 / 41.6.4 /
        Appendix A. Build a configured
        MeshStandardMaterial for a family
        variant. `family` is one of
        `'plate' | 'cloth' | 'leather' |
        'stone'` (or a custom family name
        when `opts.variants` is provided).
        `opts.variant` is the variant name
        within the family (e.g.
        `'plateDark'`, `'clothFaded'`); the
        default is the family's base variant
        (e.g. `'plate'`, `'cloth'`).
        `opts.noise` (number 0-1) enables
        the procedural color jitter from
        41.6.4. Returns a configured
        MeshStandardMaterial ready to
        attach to a Mesh. The 4 default
        families (plate, cloth, leather,
        stone) ship with the 4 default
        variants each (16 materials
        total) — see 41.6.1.

    - `detailInventory(meta)`
        PART 41.1 / Appendix C. Returns a
        structured detail-inventory object
        from a `meta` object. The shape
        is:

          {
            identity: string[],
            secondary: string[],
            tertiary: string[],
            materialZones: string[]
          }

        When `meta.detailInventory` is
        already present, the helper
        validates the shape and returns
        it (or a synthesized empty
        inventory when the input is
        missing). When the input has no
        `detailInventory`, the helper
        returns an empty inventory
        (`{ identity: [], secondary: [],
        tertiary: [], materialZones: [] }`).
        The renderer's E22 validator uses
        the same function so the
        factory's authoring-time call and
        the renderer's runtime call see
        the same shape.

    These are pure additions to the
    existing `__threeTriHelpers` bundle —
    the existing 5 PART 35 helpers, the
    3 PART 39 helpers, and the 3 PART 40
    helpers stay untouched. The
    renderer's runtime contract update is
    `lblSpec.helpers.materialVariants`
    and `lblSpec.helpers.detailInventory`
    become the canonical way to do these
    two things in a .ts factory; the
    factory does NOT need to copy the
    function bodies.

  WHAT PART 41 DOES NOT INTRODUCE
  --------------------------------

    - No new mandatory field on any existing
      userData namespace (the new fields in
      41.1 `detailInventory` and 41.3.1
      `minMeshes` are OPTIONAL; the current
      renderer ignores unknown keys safely)
    - No new export path (the existing GLB /
      OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE / USDZ / .ts
      paths are unchanged)
    - No new dependency (the new helpers use
      only Three.js APIs that are already
      imported; no import-map change)
    - No new validator that blocks the model
      from loading (E22, E23 are non-blocking,
      like E1-E21 before them)
    - No change to the existing PART 1-40
      rules (PART 41 reinforces them — see
      the 41.0 + 41.10 cross-references)
    - No change to the existing E1-E21
      validators (they stay exactly as they
      were in v1.17 / v8.9)
    - No change to PART 38.22's style
      agnosticism (PART 41 is also style-
      agnostic; the density rules apply to
      every style)
    - No change to PART 40.6.2's seeded
      random pattern (PART 41.6.4 uses the
      same helper)
    - No change to PART 40.2's 4-stage
      pipeline (PART 41 promotes it to 5
      stages by adding "Surface Detail" as
      a new stage between Form Density and
      Materials)
    - No change to the project's "Low Poly 3D"
      name

  CROSS-REFERENCES
  -----------------

    PART 41 layers on top of, never replaces:
      - PART 1  (factory contract)        — TS path
      - PART 2  (primitives)              — TS path
      - PART 3  (materials)               — TS path

    The maintainer's "System High-Detail Update
    Plan" (2026-08) is the source doc. The plan
    distills 7 prompt rules (41.8) and 6
    vocabulary patterns (41.4) that close the
    density gap between current ~20 KB models
    and higher-end >60 KB models.

================================================================================
END OF PART 41 — v1.18 / v8.10 ADDITIONS: HIGH-DETAIL GENERATION
Prefer geometric density over code brevity. A 5-stage pipeline
(Blockout → Structure → Density → Surface → Polish) that promotes
PART 40's 4-stage to a finer 5-stage. A detail inventory before any
geometry is written (identity / secondary / tertiary / material zones).
Minimum mesh count targets (200-400 for characters, 150-350 for
vehicles, 100-200 for weapons, 60-150 for props, 150-300 for
architecture). Stronger geometry vocabulary (overlapping boxes for
layered armor, multiple thin cylinders for rings, small spheres for
rivets, thin boxes for seams, slightly rotated pieces for organic
feel, multi-layer cloth for capes). A first-class silhouette and
proportion pass with multi-angle readability + limb thickness +
weight distribution + prop scale. Material variants per family
(plate / cloth / leather / stone × 4 variants each = 16 materials)
plus 3 dedicated scratch / damage / engrave materials. A 4-child
hierarchy with named nodes + canonical sockets + complete
`userData.sculptRuntime`. Adds E22 detail-inventory compliance
and E23 minimum-mesh-count target to the existing 21 non-blocking
validators. Re-exports `materialVariants` and `detailInventory` on
`window.lblSpec.helpers` alongside the PART 35 + PART 39 + PART 40
bundle. Style-agnostic, applies to every .ts factory, every JSON/JS
blueprint, every subject. No new dependency, no new export path, no
new mandatory field, no removed / weakened rule.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 42 — v1.19 / v8.11 ADDITIONS: CHARACTER PRODUCTION DISCIPLINE
================================================================================
Source: the maintainer's "3D Character Production Plan — Lead 3D
Technical Director" (2026-08). The plan is a worked example for a
stylized action hero with armor, cape, and hair, distilling the 7
production techniques, the 60/25/15 polygon distribution rule, the
11-step production pipeline (blockout → sculpt → retopo → UV →
bake → texture → hair/cloth → LODs → rig → export), the 85-bone
skeleton reference, the LOD chain LOD0-LOD4, the half-budget cut
list, the film-quality upgrade list, and the 7-risk register.

PART 42 layers on top of — never replaces — PART 1, PART 2, PART 3,
PART 4, PART 17, PART 27, PART 30, PART 31, PART 32, PART 34, PART 35,
PART 36, PART 37, PART 38, PART 38.22, PART 39, PART 40, PART 41,
PART 64, PART 65, and PART 66. PART 42 reinforces PART 41's
density-first discipline with a CHARACTER-SPECIFIC framing — a
character is a 7-technique + 11-step + 5-LOD discipline, not a
single-shot block of geometry. PART 42 also reinforces PART 31's
character sculpt (it provides the per-region discipline that
PART 31 left to the AI's authoring loop) and PART 38's character
pipeline upgrade (it provides the production-time framing that
PART 38 left to the AI's authoring loop).

  42.0  CENTRAL PRINCIPLE
  -----------------------

    A character is NOT a single-shot prompt-to-geometry call.
    A character is the OUTPUT of 7 production techniques +
    an 11-step production pipeline + a 5-LOD chain + an
    85-bone skeleton + a deformation-risk register + a
    performance budget.

    The 7 PRODUCTION TECHNIQUES (42.1) are the building
    blocks: every character surface gets exactly one
    primary technique and 0-2 secondary techniques, picked
    by region and surface type:

      1. UV mapping + image textures
      2. PBR texturing
      3. Procedural textures
      4. Vertex colors
      5. Triplanar mapping
      6. Baking (high-poly → low-poly)
      7. Texture atlasing

    The 11-STEP PIPELINE (42.3) is the production order:
    blockout → sculpt primary → sculpt secondary → retopo
    → UV → bake → texture → hair/cloth → LODs → rig → export.
    Each step has a QC gate before the next step starts.

    The 5-LOD CHAIN (42.5) is the runtime discipline: LOD0
    (35k tris) for close-up, LOD1 (18k) for mid-range, LOD2
    (7.5k) for far, LOD3 (2.5k) for background, LOD4 (100
    tris + 1k billboard) for crowd.

    The 60/25/15 polygon DISTRIBUTION (42.2) is the
    allocation rule: silhouette gets 60% of the budget,
    deformation gets 25%, detail gets 15%. A model that
    ships with 50% on detail has 20% stolen from silhouette
    and reads worse at 10m distance.

    PART 42 is style-agnostic. The 7 techniques, the 11
    steps, and the 5 LODs apply to low-poly, mid-poly,
    high-poly, smooth-shaded, stylized, photoreal, voxel,
    hand-painted, toon / cel, retro-PSX, hologram,
    wireframe, and any combination. The numeric budgets
    (35k LOD0, 18k LOD1, 7.5k LOD2, 2.5k LOD3, 100 LOD4)
    are the STYLIZED-REALISM defaults; the AI scales them
    up for film quality (PART 42.8) or down for mobile
    (PART 42.7).

  42.1  THE 7 PRODUCTION TECHNIQUES
  ---------------------------------

    Every character surface gets exactly one PRIMARY
    technique from this list, picked by the surface's role
    in the production. A surface that needs more than one
    gets a SECONDARY technique (always fewer than the
    primary). The 7 techniques are:

  42.1.1  UV mapping + image textures (primary for face,
          skin, hair cards, logo decals, fabric base colors).

    Use when: the surface is a FOCAL AREA (the face), needs
    hand-painted control (the insignia), or has high
    per-pixel detail (the hair alpha). The baked maps give
    the highest control over a focal area at minimal cost.

    Don't use when: the surface is a large flat panel with
    no focal point (use triplanar from 42.1.5 instead) or
    the surface is procedurally infinite (use procedural
    from 42.1.3 instead).

  42.1.2  PBR texturing (primary for ALL surfaces — the
          default).

    Use when: in doubt, PBR. The physically based shading
    reads correctly under any lighting condition and
    matches the rest of the scene's lights without per-
    material hacks. Author the 5 PBR maps (albedo, normal,
    metallic, roughness, AO; height is optional) via
    Substance Painter or the equivalent.

    Don't use when: the surface is voxel-style (use flat
    color) or hand-painted (use UV + image with no
    metallic/roughness).

  42.1.3  Procedural textures (primary for skin pores,
          fabric weave, metal micro-scratch, leather grain,
          dirt/wear masks).

    Use when: the surface needs INFINITELY TILEABLE, FREE-
    OF-SEAMS detail. Procedural scales to LOD0..LOD3
    without re-painting; baked UV textures need re-baking
    for each LOD.

    Don't use when: the surface needs a focal hand-painted
    detail (use UV + image from 42.1.1 instead).

  42.1.4  Vertex colors (primary for damage/weathering
          tint, dirt accumulation in crevices, per-region
          masks on the face, cape tip darkening, decal
          blend weights; secondary for low-poly AO back-up
          when no AO map exists).

    Use when: the per-region mask is needed for material
    blending (cloth vs skin vs metal) without sampling
    extra texture channels, OR when the surface is low-
    poly and an AO map would be too expensive.

    Don't use when: the surface is dense (vertex colors
    interpolate across many verts and lose the focal
    detail) or the surface needs the per-pixel control of
    a texture.

  42.1.5  Triplanar mapping (primary for cape back, large
          planar armor panels where UV stretching would
          show at grazing angles, terrain-crawling
          rock/ground contact points, hair cap underside).

    Use when: the surface is LARGE + FLAT-ISH + HERO
    CAMERA EXPOSURE. Triplanar projects XYZ blended by
    normal, so there are no UV seams and no axial
    stretching.

    Don't use when: the surface is small (3 texture
    samples is too expensive for a rivet) or the surface
    is on a curved part where the 3-axis blend would
    create visible discontinuities (use UV unwrap from
    42.1.1 instead).

  42.1.6  Baking (high-poly → low-poly normal/AO/curvature/
          ID/position bake, via xNormal or Marmoset
          Toolbag).

    Use when: the character has a HIGH-POLY SCULPT
    (1-2M tris) that captures micro-detail (rivets,
    fabric weave, leather grain, skin pores, hair strand
    direction cues, dust in crevices) and a LOW-POLY
    GAME MESH (35k target) that needs the same detail
    via normal/AO maps. One bake pass replaces hundreds
    of hand-painted hours.

    Don't use when: the character has no high-poly sculpt
    (a poly-only model) — there's nothing to bake from.

  42.1.7  Texture atlasing (primary for small props: belt
          buckles, rivets, buckles, pouches, buttons,
          insignia, gear; shared materials across identical
          gear pieces; modular armor kit).

    Use when: the surface is SMALL (rivets, buckles,
    insignia) OR the same material is used on N distinct
    meshes (modular armor kit, weapon parts). One draw
    call, one material slot per atlas = huge batching win
    on mobile/WebGL where draw calls dominate frame time.

    Don't use when: the surface is a hero zone (face,
    cape) that needs a dedicated texture set.

  42.2  THE 60/25/15 POLYGON DISTRIBUTION
  --------------------------------------

    For a stylized-realism character at LOD0 (35k tris),
    the AI allocates the budget by ROLE:

      - 60% (21k tris) — SILHOUETTE
        The parts of the model that contribute to the
        256x256 black-on-white silhouette test (PART
        39.4.1 / 41.5.1). On the worked example, this
        includes the hair cards, cape, boots, legs, arms,
        torso, pauldrons, cape hem, and weapon.
      - 25% (8.75k tris) — DEFORMATION
        The parts of the model that bend with animation
        (face, neck, hands, joint caps). The 25% covers
        the high-density face (4,200 tris), the hands
        (1,400 x 2 = 2,800), and the joint caps (1,470).
      - 15% (5.25k tris) — DETAIL
        The parts of the model that ONLY exist at close-up
        (belt segments, pouches, vambrace segments,
        greaves, boot buckles, insignia, chest inset, cape
        clasp, sash). At 10m distance the detail is below
        the 1-pixel threshold and contributes to the
        silhouette instead.

    The 60/25/15 split is the STYLIZED-REALISM default.
    For other styles:

      - LOW-POLY: 80/15/5 (silhouette dominates; detail
        is silhouette + a tiny amount of additional form).
      - HIGH-POLY: 50/30/20 (deformation gets more head-
        room for the 50k+ vertex targets from PART 38.13).
      - PHOTOREAL: 45/30/25 (deformation + detail both
        expand; silhouette shrinks to 45%).
      - VOXEL: 95/5/0 (silhouette is everything; no
        smooth deformation; no detail).
      - HAND-PAINTED: 70/20/10 (silhouette + deformation
        with hand-painted focal detail).

    The renderer's E25 polygon distribution validator
    (42.9) checks the actual silhouette/deformation/detail
    split against the declared `meta.polyDistribution`
    field; a model that ships with 30% on detail has
    stolen 15% from silhouette and reads worse at
    distance.

  42.3  THE 11-STEP PRODUCTION PIPELINE
  ------------------------------------

    A character goes through 11 STEPS, each with a QC
    gate before the next step starts. The 11 steps
    supersede the 5-stage pipeline from PART 41.2 for
    CHARACTER subjects; the 5-stage still applies to
    non-character subjects (props, vehicles, architecture).

    Step 1 — BLOCKOUT (T-pose, 4k tris total)
    Step 2 — SCULPT, PRIMARY FORMS (ZBrush, ~150k tris)
    Step 3 — SCULPT, SECONDARY DETAIL (ZBrush, 1-2M tris)
    Step 4 — RETOPOLOGY (Blender, target 35k tris)
    Step 5 — UV LAYOUT (Blender/RizomUV)
    Step 6 — BAKE (Marmoset Toolbag or xNormal)
    Step 7 — TEXTURE (Substance Painter)
    Step 8 — HAIR + CLOTH SETUP
    Step 9 — LODS (Simplygon or manual)
    Step 10 — RIG + SKIN
    Step 11 — FINAL EXPORT + ENGINE IMPORT

    Each step has a QC gate. The most important:

      - Step 1 QC: orthographic side view shows hero-
        proportion head (7.5-heads tall). Front view
        shows symmetry (max 2% deviation left vs right).
      - Step 2 QC: silhouette test — black silhouette
        render matches concept at 90%+ accuracy.
      - Step 3 QC: turn off Albedo, only AO + normal —
        does it still read?
      - Step 4 QC: no n-gons, no non-manifold edges, all
        quads except poles at anatomical landmarks.
      - Step 5 QC: checkered test render with seams
        hidden, no stretching.
      - Step 6 QC: bake compare in Marmoset, "difference"
        view < 5% error.
      - Step 7 QC: shader ball test under 3 lighting
        conditions (HDRI, point light raking, flat
        ambient).
      - Step 8 QC: hair doesn't intersect face from any
        standard camera, cape has no Z-fighting with body.
      - Step 9 QC: silhouette test at each LOD — does it
        still read?
      - Step 10 QC: deformation test (extreme poses)
        shows no collapse at shoulder, no poke-through
        at hip.
      - Step 11 QC: import in-engine, check normals
        direction, material assignment, LOD swap distance,
        draw call count.

    The 11 steps map to the 5-stage pipeline from
    PART 41.2 as:

      PART 41 Stage 1 (Blockout)        = 42.3 Step 1
      PART 41 Stage 2 (Structure)        = 42.3 Step 4
                                            (retopo)
      PART 41 Stage 3 (Form Density)     = 42.3 Step 4
                                            (retopo)
                                            + Step 6 (bake)
      PART 41 Stage 4 (Surface Detail)   = 42.3 Step 3
                                            (sculpt
                                            secondary)
      PART 41 Stage 5 (Materials &
                       Polish)          = 42.3 Step 7
                                            (texture)
                                            + Step 8 (hair/
                                            cloth)
                                            + Step 11 (export)

  42.4  RIG AND ANIMATION PREP
  ----------------------------

    The 85-bone skeleton is the production reference for
    hero characters. The 85-bone count is a healthy middle
    ground: AAA push is 120+ bones (more fingers, more
    face controls), mobile is 50-60 bones (no fingers,
    no face). The 85-bone skeleton is structured as:

      Root (1)
        Pelvis (1)
          Spine_01-03 (3)
            Neck (1)
              Head (1)
                Eye_L, Eye_R (2)
                Jaw (1)
                Tongue_01-03 (3)
            Clavicle_L, Clavicle_R (2)
              UpperArm_L, UpperArm_R (2)
                Elbow_L, Elbow_R (2)
                  Forearm_L, Forearm_R (2)
                    Wrist_L, Wrist_R (2)
                      Hand_L, Hand_R (2)
                        Thumb_01-03 x 2 (6)
                        Index_01-03 x 2 (6)
                        Middle_01-03 x 2 (6)
                        Ring_01-03 x 2 (6)
                        Pinky_01-03 x 2 (6)
            UpperLeg_L, UpperLeg_R (2)
              Knee_L, Knee_R (2)
                LowerLeg_L, LowerLeg_R (2)
                  Ankle_L, Ankle_R (2)
                    Ball_L, Ball_R (2)
                      Toe_L, Toe_R (2)
        Cape_Root (1)
          Cape_01-03 (3)
            Cape_Hem_L, Cape_Hem_R (2)

    Total: ~85 bones. The factory publishes the bone
    hierarchy on `userData.sculptRuntime.bones` and
    `userData.sculptRuntime.boneCount` so the renderer
    can attach the rig inspector and the E25 validator
    (42.9) can check the bone count against the
    subject-class floor (PART 41.3.1 / 42.5).

  42.4.1  The 7 deformation risk areas.

    Characters have 7 KNOWN deformation risk areas that
    fail in extreme poses. The AI's authoring-time audit
    (PART 39.6) checks all 7:

      1) SHOULDER/AXILLA COLLAPSE — fix with 3-loop fan
         at shoulder + stretchy helper bone + corrective
         blend shape for >120 deg arm raise.
      2) HIP/LEG PINCH — fix with clean radial loops at
         hip joint (10-12 quads meeting at crotch pole)
         + thigh-stretch bone.
      3) KNEE POLE DEFORM — fix with 8-sided radial loop
         around knee, pole at FRONT of knee (out of
         compression zone).
      4) ELBOW CREASE PINCH — fix with 3 loops tightly
         packed at elbow, pole at INNER elbow (compression
         side).
      5) CAPE INTERSECTION WITH LEGS — fix with 3 cape
         segments + progressive collider ignore distance
         (top vs spine, middle vs upper legs, bottom vs
         lower legs) + 5cm separation.
      6) HEAD ROTATION — fix with neck loops wrapping
         fully (8 sides) + 2 extra loops around the base
         to absorb the twist.
      7) BELT FOLDS IN HIP ROTATION — fix with 1-2 extra
         edge loops + weight-painted 70% pelvis + 30%
         upper leg.

    The renderer's E24 production-completeness validator
    (42.9) checks the factory's `meta.deformationFixes`
    array against the 7 risks; a model that ships without
    a `meta.deformationFixes` entry is exempt (the AI
    ran no audit), a model that ships with an entry
    surfaces the missing risks as warnings.

  42.4.2  Facial expressions and special controls.

    Hero characters need 30+ BLEND SHAPES for facial
    expression:

      - Brows: 3 (inner-up, outer-up, furrow)
      - Eyes: 5 (blink-L, blink-R, blink-both, squint,
        wide)
      - Mouth: 10 (smile, frown, open, pucker, sneer-L,
        sneer-R, corner-up-L, corner-up-R, jaw-open,
        lips-back)
      - Cheeks: 2 (puff, suck)
      - Nose: 2 (flare, sneer-nose)
      - Phonemes: 10 (A, E, I, O, U, M-B-P, F-V, L, S-T,
        W-OO; this list can be larger for non-English
        languages)

    Plus 6 PROCEDURAL CORRECTIVE shapes (arm-raise,
    knee-bend, hand-fist, finger-curl, lean-F, lean-B,
    lean-L, lean-R — 8 actually) and 6 CUSTOM RIG
    CONTROLS (Aim_Eye_L, Aim_Eye_R, Aim_Weapon, Foot_L,
    Foot_R, Hand_L, Hand_R, Spine_IK, Cape_Strength,
    Hair_Physics_Strength, Voice_Mouth_Open,
    Voice_Smile, Voice_Brow).

    The factory publishes the blend shape list on
    `userData.sculptRuntime.blendShapes` and the
    custom rig controls on
    `userData.sculptRuntime.rigControls`. Both are
    OPTIONAL — non-character subjects don't need them.

  42.5  THE 5-LOD CHAIN
  ---------------------

    The 5-LOD chain is the runtime discipline for hero
    characters. Each LOD is named, has a tri count, and
    has a use-case. The 5 LODs are:

      - LOD0 — 35,000 tris
        Full detail, all hero textures (2k face, 1k
        body). Used 0-15m from camera, close dialogue,
        cinematics.
      - LOD1 — 18,000 tris (49% reduction)
        Drop cape hem detail, drop vambrace segmenting,
        simplify buckle, drop hand fingers to 3-segment
        stubs. Keep all hero textures, swap 2k face for
        1k face. Used 15-30m, mid-range gameplay.
      - LOD2 — 7,500 tris (78% reduction)
        Drop cape to 1-segment strip, drop insignia,
        simplify hair to 2 cards, drop boot buckles.
        Drop face to 512 px, body to 512 px. Used
        30-60m, far view in crowd, vehicles.
      - LOD3 — 2,500 tris (93% reduction)
        Merged silhouette only. Single 256 px atlas, no
        normal map. Cape = single quad. Hair = single
        texture on scalp cap. Hands = mitts (no fingers).
        Used 60m+, background, impostor swap candidate.
      - LOD4 (impostor) — 100 tris + 1k billboard texture
        Single quat-camera-facing plane, baked from 8
        viewing angles. Used 100m+, crowd scenes.

    The factory publishes the LOD chain on
    `userData.sculptRuntime.lodChain`:

      meta.lodChain = [
        { level: 0, triangles: 35000, textureDensity: '2k+1k', use: 'close-up' },
        { level: 1, triangles: 18000, textureDensity: '1k+1k', use: 'mid-range' },
        { level: 2, triangles:  7500, textureDensity: '512',   use: 'far' },
        { level: 3, triangles:  2500, textureDensity: '256',   use: 'background' },
        { level: 4, triangles:   100, textureDensity: '1k-billboard', use: 'crowd' }
      ]

    The factory's PART 40.6.3 LOD support is the
    implementation hook; PART 42 adds the 5-LOD chain
    as the standard shape.

    The renderer's E25 polygon distribution validator
    (42.9) checks the LOD chain against the 60/25/15
    silhouette/deformation/detail split at LOD0.

  42.6  PERFORMANCE BUDGET
  -----------------------

    For a hero character on the AAA / PC + console
    target (60 fps, 1080p), the per-frame budget is
    ~1.4 ms / character:

      - Vertex shader:     0.6 ms
      - Fragment shader:   0.8 ms
      - Draw calls:        3 (one per active LOD)
      - Texture bandwidth: ~8 MB / frame
      - Triangles:         35k x 1 = 35k
      - TOTAL:             ~1.4 ms / character

    Headroom for 4 characters visible: ~5.6 ms (out
    of 16.67). Reasonable for an action scene with
    4 heroes on-screen.

    The WebGL fallback budget caps at LOD2 (7.5k tris)
    with a single atlas and no per-pixel cloth sim;
    targets 30 fps on integrated GPUs. The mobile
    budget is a separate build at 12k tris LOD0 / 5k
    LOD1 / 2k LOD2 with no cape (painted-on) and no
    card hair (scalp + cap only).

    The renderer's PART 38.16 / E16 bounding-box
    proportions check is the runtime equivalent of
    the performance budget's "per-frame ms" check;
    a model that ships outside the 0.5-20 X-size
    range (PART 39.1.2) will perform outside the
    1.4 ms budget on a single hero.

  42.7  THE HALF-BUDGET CUT LIST
  -----------------------------

    If the polygon budget is cut in half (35k → 17.5k),
    the AI cuts in this priority order:

      1) Cape: 920 → 280 (single 8-quad strip, no hem
         detail). Saves 640.
      2) Hair cards: 240 → 120 (drop 1 of 3 side
         cards). Saves 120.
      3) Hand fingers: 1,400 x 2 → 800 x 2 (drop
         pinky to stub). Saves 1,200.
      4) Vambrace segments: 400 x 2 → 200 x 2 (1
         plate instead of 3). Saves 400.
      5) Greave segments: 350 x 2 → 200 x 2. Saves
         300.
      6) Boot detail: 600 x 2 → 200 x 2. Saves 800.
      7) Belt buckle + pouches: 900 → 300. Saves
         600.
      8) Weapon parts: 1,800 → 1,000 (drop scope,
         sling). Saves 800.
      9) Torso simplification: 2,800 → 1,800 (drop
         chest inset bevel). Saves 1,000.
      10) Pauldrons: 700 each → 500 each. Saves 400.
      11) Head: 4,200 → 2,400 (sacrifice cheek
          topology density, keep mouth and eyes).
          Saves 1,800.
      12) Body silhouette: 3,200 → 1,800. Saves
          1,400.

    Subtotal cut: ~9,460. Brings to ~25,540. The AI
    stops here for stylized-realism; further cuts
    drop into mid-poly territory (PART 41.3.1's
    character floor of 200 meshes still applies but
    the per-region budget shifts).

    The factory publishes the cut list on
    `meta.halfBudgetCutList` (a string array, OPTIONAL)
    so the renderer's E24 production-completeness
    validator (42.9) can audit which cuts the AI
    actually took vs which the user requested.

    The NEVER CUT list (mandatory at any budget):

      - Mouth and eye topology (must deform).
      - Hip and knee loops (must deform for walk).
      - Cape attachment ring (must stay attached to
        body).

  42.8  FILM-QUALITY UPGRADE LIST
  ------------------------------

    Going from 35k (game) to ~2M (film), the AI adds
    10 items:

      1) Hair: groom with 50k-150k strand curves, real
         simulation with wind, body collision, environment
         collision. Replaces cards. Adds ~30 min of sim
         time per shot.
      2) Skin: full subsurface scattering (3-layer SSS),
         pore normal at 0.1 mm scale, micro-vessels,
         freckle procedural layer, separate head skin and
         body skin (different pore density).
      3) Eyes: real cornea with refraction, iris with
         parallax depth, moisture layer, vein procedural,
         eyelash groom.
      4) Cloth: full Marvelous Designer simulation, 30+
         poses baked into a pose library, swapped per
         shot.
      5) Armor: micro-scratch detail at 0.01 mm scale,
         fingerprint oils on grip, dust accumulation
         shader.
      6) Weapon: full mechanical detail, internal gears,
         scratches that follow ejection pattern, oil
         sheen.
      7) Topology: subdivide face to 50k tris for film
         renders.
      8) Texel density: face goes to 4096 px/m, body to
         2048.
      9) AOVs: 30+ render passes for compositing
         (diffuse, specular, SSS, coat, sheen, AO,
         contact shadow, transmission, emission, etc.).
      10) Animation polish: full mocap cleanup, hand-
          keyed fingers for close-ups, breath layer,
          micro-expression layer, weight shift for each
          pose.

    The factory publishes the film-quality items on
    `meta.filmQualityItems` (a string array, OPTIONAL).
    The renderer's E24 production-completeness validator
    checks the AI's authoring-time audit against the
    10 items; a model that ships with
    `meta.targetQuality: "film"` but without a
    `meta.filmQualityItems` entry surfaces a warning.

  42.9  RENDERER-SIDE RUNTIME CONTRACT
  ------------------------------------

    PART 42 adds 2 new non-blocking validators to the
    existing 23:

      - E24 — Production completeness (PART 42.3 / 42.4.1
        / 42.8). For character subjects (per PART 41.1's
        identity category), the factory's `meta` should
        include at least one of the 11 production steps
        (PART 42.3) AND the 7 deformation fixes (PART
        42.4.1) AND the 10 film-quality items (PART 42.8
        if `meta.targetQuality: "film"`). The validator
        surfaces missing items as warnings. Non-character
        subjects are exempt; models without
        `meta.subjectClass === "character"` are also
        exempt.

      - E25 — Polygon distribution compliance (PART
        42.2 / 42.5). If the model declares
        `meta.polyDistribution = { silhouette, deformation,
        detail }` (percentages summing to 100), the
        actual silhouette/deformation/detail split
        (computed by `polyDistribution(root)` from the
        actual vertex positions) should match within
        ±10% on each axis. Non-blocking; the model
        still loads.

    All 2 are non-blocking; the model still loads, the
    user sees a toast + a red spec chip.

    What PART 42 does NOT change in the renderer
    runtime:

      - No change to E1-E23 (they stay exactly as they
        were in v1.18 / v8.10).
      - No change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE /
        USDZ / .ts export pipeline.
      - No change to the import map.
      - No change to the loader.
      - No change to the file-picker UI.
      - No change to the scene tree or the Rig / Tris /
        Sockets buttons.
      - No new mandatory field on any existing userData
        namespace — the new fields from 42.3
        (`productionStep`), 42.4.1 (`deformationFixes`),
        42.5 (`lodChain`), 42.7 (`halfBudgetCutList`),
        42.8 (`filmQualityItems`), 42.4.2
        (`blendShapes`, `rigControls`), 42.2
        (`polyDistribution`), 42.6 (`targetQuality`) are
        ALL OPTIONAL.
      - No new dependency.

  42.10 CANONICAL HELPERS
  -----------------------

    PART 42 adds 2 new canonical helpers to the existing
    `__threeTriHelpers` bundle (PART 35 + PART 39 +
    PART 40 + PART 41). The .ts factory can call any of
    them via `window.lblSpec.helpers.<name>` without
    copying the function body.

    - `polyDistribution(root)`
        PART 42.2 / Appendix A. Compute the actual
        silhouette / deformation / detail split of a
        loaded model. The heuristic:

          - SILHOUETTE: meshes with `castShadow: true`
            AND no skeletal animation (i.e. not bound
            to a bone). Default: 60%.
          - DEFORMATION: meshes that ARE bound to a
            bone (`mesh.isSkinnedMesh === true` or
            `mesh.userData.sculptBoundTo !== null`).
            Default: 25%.
          - DETAIL: meshes with neither flag. Default:
            15%.

        Returns
        `{ silhouette, deformation, detail, total, ok }`
        where each axis is a percentage (0-100) and
        `ok` is true when all three sum to 100 within
        ±0.5. The renderer's E25 validator uses the
        same function.

    - `productionChecklist(meta)`
        PART 42.3 / 42.4 / 42.5 / 42.7 / 42.8 /
        Appendix C. Build a structured production
        checklist from a `meta` object. The shape:

          {
            steps: { blockout, sculptPrimary, sculptSecondary, retopo, uv, bake, texture, hairCloth, lods, rig, export },
            deformationFixes: { shoulder, hip, knee, elbow, cape, head, belt },
            lodChain: [ { level, triangles, ... } ],
            halfBudgetCutList: string[],
            filmQualityItems: string[],
            targetQuality: 'game' | 'film' | 'mobile',
            completeness: 0..1
          }

        The helper reads from `meta.productionStep`
        (which is the current Step 1-11 the AI is
        on), `meta.deformationFixes`, `meta.lodChain`,
        `meta.halfBudgetCutList`, `meta.filmQualityItems`,
        and `meta.targetQuality`, normalizes them, and
        returns the structured shape. The `completeness`
        field is a 0-1 score: 1.0 means all 11 steps
        are checked, all 7 deformation fixes are
        documented, the LOD chain has 5 entries, and
        (if `targetQuality === 'film'`) all 10 film-
        quality items are listed. The renderer's E24
        production-completeness validator uses the
        same function so the factory's authoring-time
        call and the renderer's runtime call see the
        same score.

    These are pure additions to the existing
    `__threeTriHelpers` bundle — the existing 5 PART
    35 helpers, the 3 PART 39 helpers, the 3 PART 40
    helpers, and the 2 PART 41 helpers stay untouched.
    The renderer's runtime contract update is
    `lblSpec.helpers.polyDistribution` and
    `lblSpec.helpers.productionChecklist` become the
    canonical way to do these two things in a .ts
    factory; the factory does NOT need to copy the
    function bodies.

  WHAT PART 42 DOES NOT INTRODUCE
  --------------------------------

    - No new mandatory field on any existing userData
      namespace (the new fields in 42.3
      `productionStep`, 42.4.1 `deformationFixes`,
      42.4.2 `blendShapes` / `rigControls`, 42.5
      `lodChain`, 42.7 `halfBudgetCutList`, 42.8
      `filmQualityItems`, 42.2 `polyDistribution`,
      42.6 `targetQuality` are ALL OPTIONAL; the
      current renderer ignores unknown keys safely)
    - No new export path (the existing GLB / OBJ /
      STL / FBX / DAE / USDZ / .ts paths are
      unchanged)
    - No new dependency (the new helpers use only
      Three.js APIs that are already imported; no
      import-map change)
    - No new validator that blocks the model from
      loading (E24, E25 are non-blocking, like E1-E23
      before them)
    - No change to the existing PART 1-41 rules
      (PART 42 reinforces them — see the 42.0 +
      42.10 cross-references)
    - No change to the existing E1-E23 validators
      (they stay exactly as they were in v1.18 / v8.10)
    - No change to PART 38.22's style agnosticism
      (PART 42 is also style-agnostic — applies to
      every subject, every style, every path; the
      7-technique + 11-step + 5-LOD framing is style-
      agnostic; only the numeric budgets change with
      style)
    - No change to PART 41's 5-stage pipeline
      (PART 42's 11-step pipeline is the
      CHARACTER-SPECIFIC superset; non-character
      subjects still use PART 41's 5-stage)
    - No change to the project's "Low Poly 3D" name
