    (2026-08) is the source doc. The plan distills
    7 production techniques (42.1), 60/25/15 polygon
    distribution (42.2), 11-step pipeline (42.3),
    85-bone skeleton (42.4), 5-LOD chain (42.5),
    half-budget cut list (42.7), and film-quality
    upgrade list (42.8) as a single character-specific
    discipline that closes the gap between the existing
    PART 31 character sculpt (which provides the
    authoring-time framing) and a production-ready
    hero character.

================================================================================
END OF PART 42 — v1.19 / v8.11 ADDITIONS: CHARACTER PRODUCTION DISCIPLINE
A character is a 7-technique + 11-step + 5-LOD + 85-bone + 7-deformation-
risk + half-budget-cut-list + film-quality-upgrade-list discipline, not
a single-shot prompt-to-geometry call. The 7 production techniques:
UV + image textures, PBR texturing, procedural textures, vertex colors,
triplanar mapping, baking, texture atlasing. The 60/25/15 polygon
distribution rule: silhouette 60% / deformation 25% / detail 15%
(stylized-realism default). The 11-step pipeline: blockout → sculpt
primary → sculpt secondary → retopo → UV → bake → texture → hair/cloth
→ LODs → rig → export, each with a QC gate. The 85-bone skeleton
reference for hero characters. The 5-LOD chain: LOD0 35k / LOD1 18k /
LOD2 7.5k / LOD3 2.5k / LOD4 100 + billboard. The 7 deformation risk
areas (shoulder, hip, knee, elbow, cape, head, belt) and their fixes.
The 30+ blend shapes for facial expression. The performance budget of
~1.4 ms / character at 60 fps. The half-budget cut list (12 cuts in
priority order). The film-quality upgrade list (10 additions for going
to ~2M tris). The never-cut list (mouth / eye topology, hip / knee
loops, cape attachment). Adds E24 production-completeness (steps +
deformation fixes + film-quality items) and E25 polygon-distribution
compliance (60/25/15 split) to the existing 23 non-blocking
validators. Re-exports `polyDistribution(root)` and
`productionChecklist(meta)` on `window.lblSpec.helpers` alongside the
PART 35 + PART 39 + PART 40 + PART 41 bundle. Character-specific
extension of PART 41's 5-stage pipeline; non-character subjects still
use the PART 41 5-stage. Style-agnostic, applies to every .ts factory
+ JSON/JS blueprint, every subject, every style. No new dependency, no
new export path, no new mandatory field, no removed / weakened rule.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 43 — v1.20 / v8.12 ADDITIONS: REFINEMENT + STYLE LOCKING + VARIANT
RANKING
================================================================================
Source: the maintainer's "Techniques to Update Your System for
Better 3D Models" (2026-08). The doc distills 7 categories of
techniques the AI must apply when generating 3D models, with the
explicit goal of closing the gap to higher-end systems (Meshy 6
and Tripo's latest versions). The 7 categories are: (1) input
quality & prompt engineering, (2) multi-stage / coarse-to-fine
generation, (3) topology & mesh quality, (4) texture & material
generation, (5) geometry refinement, (6) style & consistency
controls, and (7) evaluation & feedback loop.

PART 43 layers on top of — never replaces — PART 1, PART 2, PART 3,
PART 4, PART 17, PART 27, PART 30, PART 31, PART 32, PART 34, PART 35,
PART 36, PART 37, PART 38, PART 38.22, PART 39, PART 40, PART 41,
PART 42, PART 64, PART 65, and PART 66.

Categories (1) through (4) are already covered by PART 40
(40.1 better prompt & input handling, 40.2 multi-stage generation
pipeline, 40.3 mesh quality & topology, 40.5 texture & material
system). PART 43 reinforces them with cross-references and adds
the 4 geometry refinement techniques (43.1), the 4 style &
consistency controls (43.2), and the variant ranking discipline
(43.3) that the source doc lists but the prior PARTs did not
fully cover. PART 43 is the BRIDGE between "good enough" and
"Meshy 6 / Tripo level".

  43.0  CENTRAL PRINCIPLE
  -----------------------

    PART 43 layers the 3 NEW categories from the source doc
    on top of the 4 ALREADY-COVERED categories:

      - (1) Input quality & prompt engineering
        ALREADY COVERED by PART 40.1 (40.1.1 structured
        prompts, 40.1.2 image preprocessing, 40.1.3 multi-
        view image support, 40.1.4 negative prompts and
        hard constraints). PART 43 reinforces with cross-
        references in 43.4.

      - (2) Multi-stage / coarse-to-fine generation
        ALREADY COVERED by PART 40.2 (4-stage pipeline)
        and EXTENDED by PART 41.2 (5-stage pipeline) and
        PART 42.3 (11-step character pipeline). PART 43
        reinforces with cross-references in 43.4.

      - (3) Topology & mesh quality improvements
        ALREADY COVERED by PART 40.3 (mesh cleanup, smart
        low-poly mode, feature-preserving simplification,
        watertight guarantee). PART 43 reinforces with
        cross-references in 43.4.

      - (4) Better texture & material generation
        ALREADY COVERED by PART 40.5 (PBR texture sets,
        style-specific texturing, texture refinement
        stage, automatic UV handling). PART 43 reinforces
        with cross-references in 43.4.

      - (5) Geometry refinement techniques
        NEW in PART 43 (43.1). 4 techniques the prior
        PARTs did not cover: normal-guided refinement
        (43.1.1), view-by-view iterative refinement
        (43.1.2), detail enhancement networks (43.1.3),
        and segmentation + part-aware generation (43.1.4).

      - (6) Style & consistency controls
        NEW in PART 43 (43.2). 4 controls the prior
        PARTs touched but did not centralize: strong
        style locking (43.2.1), consistent polygon
        density and edge flow (43.2.2), silhouette-
        first optimization (43.2.3), and hard-surface
        vs organic topology (43.2.4).

      - (7) Evaluation & feedback loop
        PARTIALLY COVERED by PART 40.7 (post-processing,
        validation, feedback). PART 43 EXTENDS it with
        the 3 variant-ranking techniques (43.3): generate
        N variants (43.3.1), rank by quality metrics
        (43.3.2), and a tighter feedback loop (43.3.3
        that supersedes PART 40.7.4).

    The central principle of PART 43:

      REFINEMENT is the bridge from "good enough" to
      "Meshy 6 / Tripo level". CONSISTENCY is the
      discipline that makes a model family look like it
      belongs together. RANKING is the discipline that
      gives the user the best of N, not the first of N.

    PART 43 is style-agnostic. The techniques apply to
    low-poly, mid-poly, high-poly, smooth-shaded,
    stylized, photoreal, voxel, hand-painted, toon /
    cel, retro-PSX, hologram, wireframe, and any
    combination.

  43.1  GEOMETRY REFINEMENT TECHNIQUES
  ------------------------------------

    The 4 geometry refinement techniques from the source
    doc's Section 5. Each technique is a separate
    authoring-time pass; the AI applies them in addition
    to the multi-stage pipeline from PART 40.2 / 41.2 /
    42.3.

  43.1.1  Normal-guided refinement.

    A normal map predicts the SURFACE DIRECTION of a
    region; geometry refinement uses that prediction to
    SHIFT THE VERTEX POSITIONS so the actual surface
    matches the predicted normal. The result: a model
    that LOOKS like it has more polygons than it does
    (the normal map fakes the high-frequency detail, the
    geometry carries the low-frequency form).

    The AI applies normal-guided refinement in Stage 4
    of PART 41.2 (Surface Detail) and Step 7 of PART
    42.3 (Texture). The pass:

      1. Generate a high-frequency normal map from a
         high-poly sculpt (PART 42.1.6) or a procedural
         noise source.
      2. For each vertex, compute the predicted normal
         direction (the average of the 3 adjacent face
         normals after the normal map is applied).
      3. Shift the vertex along the predicted normal by
         a small amount (typically 0.001-0.01 units, in
         the direction of the predicted normal).
      4. Re-bake the normal map from the updated vertex
         positions.
      5. Repeat steps 2-4 until the predicted normals
         match the target normal map within tolerance.

    The technique is "normal-guided" because the
    REFINEMENT DIRECTION is determined by the normal
    map, not by the AI's authoring choice. A model
    with a strong horizontal grain (like a wood plank)
    gets shifted along the grain; a model with a strong
    vertical grain (like a column) gets shifted along
    the column.

    PART 43 introduces `meta.normalRefinementPasses`
    (number, default 0, OPTIONAL) to declare how many
    passes the AI ran. The renderer's E26 style-lock
    compliance check (43.5) doesn't enforce this — it's
    an authoring-time decision.

  43.1.2  View-by-view iterative refinement.

    A model that looks great from the front may look
    bad from the back. View-by-view iterative
    refinement alternates between:

      - View 1 (front): render silhouette + texture,
        identify failure modes (silhouette breaks,
        texture is wrong, color is off).
      - Refine 1: adjust geometry, texture, or color
        to fix the failure modes.
      - View 2 (side): render silhouette + texture,
        identify failure modes.
      - Refine 2: same as Refine 1.
      - ... (continue for N views)
      - Final: combine the N refinements into a single
        mesh + texture set.

    The technique is "view-by-view" because each
    refinement is TARGETED at one view; the AI doesn't
    try to fix all views at once. The combination step
    at the end is the LEAST BAD compromise across all
    views, not the BEST for any single view.

    The PART 40.1.3 multi-view image support feeds
    this: when the user supplies 2-4 reference views,
    the AI's authoring loop is "iterate view-by-view
    until all N views pass the silhouette test". The
    factory publishes the view-by-view pass count on
    `meta.viewRefinementPasses` (number, default 0,
    OPTIONAL) and the per-view refinement log on
    `meta.viewRefinementLog` (array of { view,
    passCount, lastFailures }, OPTIONAL).

    The renderer's E27 multi-view consistency check
    (43.5) verifies the model has the declared number
    of view passes; a model with
    `meta.referenceViews.length === 4` but
    `viewRefinementPasses === 0` surfaces a warning.

  43.1.3  Detail enhancement networks.

    A specialized model that takes a low-poly mesh and
    a reference image, and adds HIGH-FREQUENCY DETAIL
    to the geometry without changing the silhouette.
    The technique is "detail enhancement" because it
    PRESERVES the silhouette (low-frequency form) and
    ADDS the detail (high-frequency form). The output
    is the same low-poly topology with shifted vertex
    positions, ready for normal-map baking (PART
    42.1.6) or direct rendering.

    The AI applies detail enhancement in Stage 3 of
    PART 41.2 (Form Density) and Step 4 of PART 42.3
    (Retopology). The pass:

      1. Take the low-poly mesh from Stage 2 / Step 2.
      2. Render a high-frequency detail target from the
         reference image (a depth map, a normal map,
         or a height map).
      3. Run a detail enhancement network that maps
         (low-poly mesh, high-frequency target) →
         (refined low-poly mesh).
      4. Verify the refined mesh still passes the
         PART 39.4.1 silhouette test (the silhouette
         should be UNCHANGED).
      5. Re-bake the normal map from the refined
         mesh.

    The factory publishes the network used (or the
    "manual" / "noise-based" alternative) on
    `meta.detailEnhancer` (string, OPTIONAL). The
    renderer's E26 style-lock check (43.5) doesn't
    enforce this — it's an authoring-time decision.

  43.1.4  Segmentation + part-aware generation.

    Auto-split the model into LOGICAL PARTS (head,
    body, weapon, etc.) BEFORE the geometry refinement
    passes. The segmentation lets the AI apply
    different techniques to different parts: a wood
    handle gets procedural grain (PART 42.1.3), a
    metal blade gets baked normal + procedural
    scratch (PART 42.1.6 + 42.1.3), a leather grip
    gets vertex-color wear (PART 42.1.4).

    The PART 41.1 detail inventory's `identity`,
    `secondary`, `tertiary`, and `materialZones`
    categories are the EXPLICIT form of segmentation
    — the AI names the parts before any geometry is
    written, and the parts map to the right
    techniques. PART 43.1.4 EXTENDS PART 41.1 with
    a SEGMENTATION HINT field that the AI publishes
    on `meta.segmentation` (array of part names,
    OPTIONAL) so the renderer can read the parts
    without parsing the detail inventory.

    The renderer's E26 style-lock check (43.5)
    verifies that all segments share the same style
    lock; a model with 3 segments using 3 different
    styles surfaces a warning.

  43.2  STYLE & CONSISTENCY CONTROLS
  ----------------------------------

    The 4 style & consistency controls from the source
    doc's Section 6. Each control is a DECLARATION
    on `meta.styleLock` that the renderer enforces.

  43.2.1  Strong style locking.

    The model declares a SINGLE style on
    `meta.styleLock` (string, one of 'pure-low-poly' |
    'stylized' | 'semi-realistic' | 'realistic' |
    'voxel' | 'hand-painted' | 'toon-cel' |
    'retro-psx' | 'hologram' | 'wireframe'). The
    renderer checks that all materials and the
    overall density / style usage match the locked
    style. A model that locks 'pure-low-poly' but
    uses a `MeshPhysicalMaterial` with a 4k normal
    map is a STYLE LOCK VIOLATION.

    PART 38.22 already establishes that the system
    supports any style and that low-poly is the
    default for short prompts. PART 43.2.1 EXTENDS
    PART 38.22 with an EXPLICIT LOCK field that
    the AI publishes when the user wants to
    commit to a single style; without the lock,
    the model can use mixed styles (PART 38.22's
    style-agnosticism).

    The renderer's E26 style-lock compliance check
    (43.5) reads `meta.styleLock` and verifies:

      - All materials match the locked style's
        material kind ('pure-low-poly' → MeshLambert
        or flat MeshStandard, no normal maps;
        'realistic' → MeshPhysical with full PBR
        set; etc.).
      - The triangle density matches the locked
        style's range (PART 42.2 — pure-low-poly
        is 80/15/5, realistic is 45/30/25).
      - The texture density matches the locked
        style's density ('pure-low-poly' is
        'low', 'realistic' is 'high').

    Models without `meta.styleLock` are exempt
    (the AI chose to be style-agnostic on purpose).

  43.2.2  Consistent polygon density and edge flow.

    When the AI is generating N models for a single
    scene (e.g. "a medieval village" with 12
    buildings, 30 villagers, 15 props), every model
    should share the SAME polygon density and edge
    flow rules. A medieval village with 12
    buildings at 2k tris each + 30 villagers at
    20k tris each is INCONSISTENT; the villagers
    would dwarf the buildings visually.

    The AI declares the family-wide density on
    `meta.familyDensity` (number, OPTIONAL) and the
    family-wide edge flow on `meta.familyEdgeFlow`
    (string, one of 'organic' | 'hard-surface' |
    'mixed', OPTIONAL). The renderer does NOT
    enforce cross-model consistency (the renderer
    sees one model at a time); the AI enforces it
    at authoring time.

    The renderer's E26 style-lock check (43.5)
    doesn't enforce family-wide density either —
    this is a discipline the AI follows when
    generating N models, not a check the renderer
    runs on a single model. The AI's authoring-time
    audit (PART 39.6) gets a new line: "is this
    model consistent with the family-wide density
    and edge flow?"

  43.2.3  Silhouette-first optimization.

    The PART 39.4.1 silhouette test (256x256 black-
    on-white, 90%+ accuracy vs reference) is the
    AUTHORING-TIME check; the PART 42.2 60%
    silhouette budget is the ALLOCATION rule.
    PART 43.2.3 EXTENDS both with a SILHOUETTE-
    PRIORITY view selection: the AI optimizes the
    silhouette from the MOST IMPORTANT camera angles
    first (typically front + 3/4 + side), then
    fills in the back / top / bottom from the
    remaining budget.

    The AI declares the priority order on
    `meta.silhouettePriority` (array of camera
    angle names, default
    ['front', '3/4-front', 'side', '3/4-back',
    'back', 'top'], OPTIONAL). The renderer's E27
    multi-view consistency check (43.5) verifies
    the declared priority order matches the actual
    `meta.referenceViews` order (when both are
    declared); a model with
    `silhouettePriority: ['front', 'side']` but
    `referenceViews: ['front', 'side', 'top']`
    surfaces a warning.

  43.2.4  Hard-surface vs organic topology.

    A hard-surface model (a robot, a vehicle, a
    weapon) has SHARP EDGES and PLANAR SURFACES.
    An organic model (a character, a creature, a
    plant) has SMOOTH EDGES and CURVED SURFACES.
    The two require different topology rules:

      - Hard-surface: edge loops at every sharp
        edge, planar surfaces on every face, no
        smooth shading unless explicitly requested.
      - Organic: edge loops following the muscle /
        bone structure, smooth shading on every
        face, no sharp edges unless explicitly
        requested.

    The AI declares the topology preference on
    `meta.topologyPreference` (string, one of
    'hard-surface' | 'organic' | 'mixed' |
    'auto-detect', OPTIONAL). The renderer does
    NOT enforce this — it's an authoring-time
    decision. The PART 38.16 / E16 bounding-box
    proportions check is the only topology check
    the renderer runs.

  43.3  VARIANT GENERATION + AUTOMATIC RANKING
  --------------------------------------------

    The 3 variant-ranking techniques from the source
    doc's Section 7. PART 40.7.4 introduced the
    user rating feedback loop; PART 43.3 EXTENDS
    it with a fuller variant ranking discipline.

  43.3.1  Generate N variants.

    When the user asks for a model without an exact
    reference (e.g. "a knight", "a medieval
    village", "a fantasy tree"), the AI generates
    N variants (default N=3, OPTIONAL override via
    `meta.variantCount`) and ships all N. Each
    variant is a complete .ts factory (or JSON
    blueprint); the user picks the best one or the
    renderer auto-ranks them.

    The N variants are generated from the same
    detail inventory (PART 41.1) and the same
    pipeline (PART 42.3) but with different
    `meta.seed` values (PART 40.6.2). The seeded
    RNG (PART 40.6.2 / 40.10) ensures the N
    variants are DETERMINISTIC — the user can
    regenerate the exact N variants on reload.

    The factory publishes the N variants as a
    `meta.variants` array (array of factory
    references or blueprint references, OPTIONAL).
    On the TS path, the variants are siblings
    inside the same file; on the JSON/JS path,
    the variants are separate top-level keys
    (`variants: [bp1, bp2, bp3]`).

  43.3.2  Rank by quality metrics.

    The renderer's E26 + E27 + the existing E1-E25
    provide the QUALITY METRICS for ranking. The
    ranking function is `lblSpec.helpers.rankVariants`
    (43.6), which scores each variant on:

      - Silhouette accuracy (PART 39.4.1, IoU vs
        reference).
      - Poly count vs target (PART 40.3.2 / E21).
      - Mesh count vs target (PART 41.3.1 / E23).
      - Density over brevity (PART 41.3 — 60/25/15
        / E25).
      - Production completeness (PART 42.3 / E24).
      - Style lock compliance (PART 43.2.1 / E26).
      - Multi-view consistency (PART 43.1.2 / E27).

    The function returns the N variants sorted by
    score (highest first). The user sees the
    top-ranked variant by default; clicking
    through to a lower-ranked variant is one click
    away.

  43.3.3  User feedback loop.

    The PART 40.7.4 user rating feedback loop is
    EXTENDED by PART 43.3.3 with TWO additional
    hooks:

      - The user can mark a variant as "favorite";
        the favorite becomes the seed for the next
        round of variant generation. The factory
        records the favorite's `meta.seed` on
        `meta.favoriteSeed` (number, OPTIONAL).
      - The user can rate a variant on a 1-5 scale
        (1 = worst, 5 = best); the rating feeds
        back into the AI's prompt defaults for the
        next session. The factory records the
        average rating on `meta.averageRating`
        (number 1-5, OPTIONAL).

    The renderer does NOT have to implement the
    favorite / rating UI in v1.20 / v8.12 — that's
    a future round. PART 43.3.3 documents the
    INTENT and the metadata fields so the AI's
    authoring-time defaults can be tuned from the
    rating data when the UI ships.

  43.4  WHAT PART 43 REINFORCES FROM PART 40-42
  --------------------------------------------

    PART 43 is layered on top of — never replaces —
    PART 40 (production-grade pipeline), PART 41
    (high-detail density), and PART 42 (character
    production discipline). The 4 categories the
    source doc lists that PART 40-42 already
    covered are reinforced with cross-references:

      - (1) Input quality & prompt engineering
        → PART 40.1 (structured prompts, image
        preprocessing, multi-view, negative
        prompts). PART 43 reinforces 43.1.2
        view-by-view iterative refinement (which
        consumes the PART 40.1.3 multi-view image
        support) and 43.1.4 segmentation (which
        consumes the PART 41.1 detail inventory).

      - (2) Multi-stage / coarse-to-fine
        generation
        → PART 40.2 (4-stage), PART 41.2
        (5-stage), PART 42.3 (11-step character).
        PART 43 reinforces 43.1.1 normal-guided
        refinement (which runs in Stage 4 of PART
        41.2) and 43.1.2 view-by-view iterative
        refinement (which runs across Stages 2-5).

      - (3) Topology & mesh quality improvements
        → PART 40.3 (cleanup, smart low-poly,
        feature-preserving, watertight). PART 43
        reinforces 43.1.3 detail enhancement
        networks (which preserve the low-poly
        topology from PART 40.3.2) and 43.2.2
        consistent polygon density (which extends
        PART 40.3.2's per-model target to the
        family-wide level).

      - (4) Better texture & material generation
        → PART 40.5 (PBR set, style-specific
        texturing, texture refinement stage,
        automatic UV handling). PART 43
        reinforces 43.1.1 normal-guided refinement
        (which consumes the normal map from PART
        40.5.1) and 43.2.1 strong style locking
        (which constrains the material kind from
        PART 40.5.1 to a single style).

    The 3 NEW categories from PART 43 (geometry
    refinement, style & consistency, variant
    ranking) are documented in 43.1 / 43.2 / 43.3
    above.

  43.5  RENDERER-SIDE RUNTIME CONTRACT
  ------------------------------------

    PART 43 adds 2 new non-blocking validators to
    the existing 25:

      - E26 — Style lock compliance (PART 43.2.1).
        If the model declares `meta.styleLock`,
        the renderer's runtime check verifies:

          - Material kind matches the locked style
            ('pure-low-poly' → no normal maps;
            'realistic' → MeshPhysical with full
            PBR; 'voxel' → flat colors only; etc.).
          - Triangle density matches the locked
            style's expected range (PART 42.2).
          - Texture density matches the locked
            style's expected density.

        A model that declares `styleLock: 'pure-
        low-poly'` but uses a 4k normal map is a
        STYLE LOCK VIOLATION. The renderer surfaces
        the violation in the warning. Models without
        `meta.styleLock` are exempt (the AI chose
        to be style-agnostic on purpose — PART
        38.22). Non-blocking.

      - E27 — Multi-view consistency (PART 40.1.3
        / 43.1.2 / 43.2.3). If the model declares
        `meta.referenceViews` (an array of view
        names from PART 40.1.3), the renderer's
        runtime check verifies:

          - The actual model has at least N named
            parts (one per view, N = the array
            length).
          - The model's `meta.silhouettePriority`
            (when declared) is consistent with the
            `meta.referenceViews` order.
          - The model's `meta.viewRefinementPasses`
            (when declared) is at least 1 (a model
            that says "I have 4 reference views"
            but "I ran 0 refinement passes" is a
            sign the AI skipped 43.1.2).

        Models without `meta.referenceViews` are
        exempt. Non-blocking.

    All 2 are non-blocking; the model still loads,
    the user sees a toast + a red spec chip. They
    mirror the E1-E25 pattern and integrate into
    the existing `validate()` / `applyToUI()` /
    `revalidate()` pipeline without any caller-side
    changes.

    What PART 43 does NOT change in the renderer
    runtime:

      - No change to E1-E25 (they stay exactly
        as they were in v1.19 / v8.11).
      - No change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX /
        DAE / USDZ / .ts export pipeline.
      - No change to the import map.
      - No change to the loader.
      - No change to the file-picker UI.
      - No change to the scene tree or the Rig /
        Tris / Sockets buttons.
      - No new mandatory field on any existing
        userData namespace — the new fields from
        43.1.1 `normalRefinementPasses`, 43.1.2
        `viewRefinementPasses` /
        `viewRefinementLog`, 43.1.3
        `detailEnhancer`, 43.1.4 `segmentation`,
        43.2.1 `styleLock`, 43.2.2
        `familyDensity` / `familyEdgeFlow`,
        43.2.3 `silhouettePriority`, 43.2.4
        `topologyPreference`, 43.3.1 `variantCount`
        / `variants`, 43.3.3 `favoriteSeed` /
        `averageRating` are ALL OPTIONAL.
      - No new dependency.

  43.6  CANONICAL HELPERS
  ----------------------

    PART 43 adds 2 new canonical helpers to the
    existing `__threeTriHelpers` bundle (PART 35
    + PART 39 + PART 40 + PART 41 + PART 42). The
    .ts factory can call any of them via
    `window.lblSpec.helpers.<name>` without copying
    the function body.

    - `viewRefinePass(views, opts)`
        PART 43.1.2 / Appendix A. Run a view-by-
        view refinement pass on a model's
        silhouette. The helper is a STUB — the
        actual refinement runs in the AI's
        authoring-time loop, not at runtime. What
        the helper does:

          - Returns a structured plan
            `{ views: [...], passesPerView:
            opts.passesPerView || 1, log: [] }`
            that the AI's authoring loop can use
            to track the per-view pass count and
            the last-known failure modes.

          - Validates the `views` array against
            the PART 40.1.3 canonical view names
            (front / 3/4-front / side / 3/4-back
            / back / top / 3/4-top / bottom).
            Unknown view names surface a console
            warning.

          - Returns the plan with a 0-1
            `completeness` score: 1.0 means the
            plan has at least 2 views AND at
            least 1 pass per view; lower scores
            surface as a console hint that the
            AI should run more passes.

        The renderer's E27 multi-view consistency
        validator uses the same function.

    - `rankVariants(variants, opts)`
        PART 43.3.2 / Appendix C. Score and rank
        N variants by quality metrics. The
        function:

          - For each variant, calls the existing
            `validate(root)` (E1-E27) to gather
            warnings.
          - Computes a score for each variant
            based on:
              - Number of warnings (lower is
                better; -1 per warning).
              - Silhouette accuracy vs the
                variant's `meta.referenceViews`
                (when declared; PART 40.1.3 / 43.1.2).
              - Poly count vs `meta.targetTris`
                (PART 40.3.2 / E21).
              - Mesh count vs `meta.minMeshes` or
                `meta.subjectClass` floor (PART
                41.3.1 / E23).
              - Poly distribution vs
                `meta.polyDistribution` (PART 42.2
                / E25).
              - Style lock compliance (PART
                43.2.1 / E26).
              - Production completeness (PART
                42.3 / E24).
          - Returns the N variants sorted by
            score (highest first) with a 0-1
            `confidence` field per variant
            (1.0 = no warnings, 0.0 = many
            warnings).

        The function does NOT actually render
        the variants (the rendering is the
        renderer's job); it returns a SORTED
        LIST that the AI / renderer uses to
        pick the top variant. The user can
        click through to a lower-ranked variant
        via the model store.

    These are pure additions to the existing
    `__threeTriHelpers` bundle — the existing
    5 PART 35 helpers, the 3 PART 39 helpers,
    the 3 PART 40 helpers, the 2 PART 41
    helpers, and the 2 PART 42 helpers stay
    untouched. The renderer's runtime contract
    update is `lblSpec.helpers.viewRefinePass`
    and `lblSpec.helpers.rankVariants` become
    the canonical way to do these two things
    in a .ts factory; the factory does NOT
    need to copy the function bodies.

  WHAT PART 43 DOES NOT INTRODUCE
  --------------------------------

    - No new mandatory field on any existing
      userData namespace (the new fields in
      43.1.1 `normalRefinementPasses`, 43.1.2
      `viewRefinementPasses` / `viewRefinementLog`,
      43.1.3 `detailEnhancer`, 43.1.4
      `segmentation`, 43.2.1 `styleLock`, 43.2.2
      `familyDensity` / `familyEdgeFlow`, 43.2.3
      `silhouettePriority`, 43.2.4
      `topologyPreference`, 43.3.1 `variantCount` /
      `variants`, 43.3.3 `favoriteSeed` /
      `averageRating` are ALL OPTIONAL; the
      current renderer ignores unknown keys
      safely)
    - No new export path (the existing GLB / OBJ
      / STL / FBX / DAE / USDZ / .ts paths are
      unchanged)
    - No new dependency (the new helpers use only
      Three.js APIs that are already imported; no
      import-map change)
    - No new validator that blocks the model from
      loading (E26, E27 are non-blocking, like
      E1-E25 before them)
    - No change to the existing PART 1-42 rules
      (PART 43 reinforces them — see the 43.0 +
      43.4 + 43.6 cross-references)
    - No change to the existing E1-E25 validators
      (they stay exactly as they were in v1.19 /
      v8.11)
    - No change to PART 38.22's style agnosticism
      (PART 43.2.1 style-lock is OPTIONAL; without
      it, the model is still style-agnostic)
    - No change to PART 41's 5-stage pipeline
      (PART 43's geometry refinement techniques
      run INSIDE the existing stages, not as new
      stages)
    - No change to PART 42's 11-step pipeline
      (PART 43's style & consistency controls are
      meta-declarations, not pipeline steps)
    - No change to the project's "Low Poly 3D"
      name

================================================================================
END OF PART 43 — v1.20 / v8.12 ADDITIONS: REFINEMENT + STYLE LOCKING +
VARIANT RANKING
The bridge from "good enough" to "Meshy 6 / Tripo level". 4 geometry
refinement techniques: normal-guided refinement (43.1.1, predict normals
and shift vertices to match), view-by-view iterative refinement
(43.1.2, alternate texture and geometry from N views), detail
enhancement networks (43.1.3, preserve silhouette + add high-frequency
detail), segmentation + part-aware generation (43.1.4, split the model
into logical parts so each part gets the right technique). 4 style &
consistency controls: strong style locking (43.2.1, `meta.styleLock`
declaration that constrains the material kind + density + texture
density to a single style), consistent polygon density and edge flow
across model families (43.2.2, `meta.familyDensity` + `meta.familyEdgeFlow`
for N-model scenes), silhouette-first optimization (43.2.3,
`meta.silhouettePriority` declaration that the AI optimizes from
important angles first), hard-surface vs organic topology
(43.2.4, `meta.topologyPreference` for sharp-edge vs smooth-edge
rules). 3 variant-ranking techniques: generate N variants from the
same detail inventory + different `meta.seed` (43.3.1), rank by
quality metrics via the E1-E27 validators (43.3.2), user feedback
loop with favorite + 1-5 rating that feeds back into the next
session's prompt defaults (43.3.3, EXTENDS PART 40.7.4). Adds E26
style-lock compliance (material kind + density + texture density
match the locked style) and E27 multi-view consistency (named parts
>= reference views, viewRefinementPasses >= 1) to the existing 25
non-blocking validators. Re-exports `viewRefinePass(views, opts)` and
`rankVariants(variants, opts)` on `window.lblSpec.helpers` alongside
the PART 35 + PART 39 + PART 40 + PART 41 + PART 42 bundle. PART 43
is style-agnostic, applies to every .ts factory + JSON/JS blueprint,
every subject, every style. PART 43 reinforces PART 40-42 with
cross-references for the 4 already-covered categories (input
quality, multi-stage, topology, textures) and ADDS the 3 new
categories (refinement, style & consistency, variant ranking). No
new dependency, no new export path, no new mandatory field, no
removed / weakened rule.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 44 — v1.21 / v8.13 ADDITIONS: PROFESSIONAL PIPELINE (GLB-FIRST +
BVH + LOOK-DEV LIGHTS + CODING RULES)
================================================================================
Source: the maintainer's "Three.js / procedural 3D character and
asset systems engineer" upgrade brief (2026-08). The brief is a
hands-on implementation roadmap: move from primitive-assembly to a
continuous-topology + GLB-first pipeline with BVH-accelerated
raycasting, per-model look-dev lights, fitted clothing/armor
layers, baked animation clips, multi-view validation renders, and
a strict house style for the .ts factory code. PART 44 layers onto
PARTs 1-43; most of the architecture is already covered by PART 38
(character pipeline upgrade, GLB-first), PART 39 (3D modeling
techniques, boot-time validator), PART 40 (production-grade
pipeline, parametric / procedural techniques), PART 41 (high-
detail generation, 5-stage pipeline, material variants), PART 42
(character production discipline, 11-step pipeline, 5-LOD chain,
deformation risks, 30+ blend shapes), and PART 43 (refinement +
style locking + variant ranking). PART 44 adds the 4 specific
gaps the brief calls out: (1) BVH-accelerated raycasting via
three-mesh-bvh (the import-map entry was already in place from
v0.6 — PART 44 ADDS the helper that uses it), (2) per-model
look-dev lights, (3) the 6-family material hierarchy with bone +
magic, and (4) the maintainer's house style for .ts factory code
(6 practical coding rules). The fitted clothing pattern and the
animation clip baking are already partially covered by PART 38
and PART 42; PART 44 EXTENDS them with the implementation
patterns the brief calls out.

PART 44 layers on top of — never replaces — PART 1, PART 2, PART 3,
PART 4, PART 17, PART 27, PART 30, PART 31, PART 32, PART 34, PART 35,
PART 36, PART 37, PART 38, PART 38.22, PART 39, PART 40, PART 41,
PART 42, PART 43, PART 64, PART 65, and PART 66.

  44.0  CENTRAL PRINCIPLE
  -----------------------

    The maintainer's brief asks for ONE architectural shift:
    the .ts factory is no longer the primary artifact — the
    GLB file is.

    A "primitive-assembly" .ts factory that builds a model
    from 200 individual `BoxGeometry` + `SphereGeometry` +
    `CylinderGeometry` calls in a deep Group hierarchy is a
    valid RUNTIME artifact (the renderer can load it,
    display it, animate it), but it is a POOR SHIPPING
    artifact (it can't be exported as a clean GLB, it has
    no skeletal skinning, it has no real materials, it
    can't be imported into Blender, it doesn't have a
    proper UV layout). PART 38.14 already established the
    GLB-first architecture; PART 44 EXTENDS it with the
    implementation patterns the brief calls out.

    The new central principle:

      SHIP THE GLB. THE .ts FACTORY IS THE VIEWER ADAPTER.

    The .ts factory does FOUR things:
      1. LOADS the GLB (via `GLTFLoader` from `three/addons/`).
      2. ATTACHES the rigged skeleton, skin weights, and
         animation mixer.
      3. EXTRACTS the sockets, the look-dev lights, the
         BVH index, and the validation surface.
      4. VALIDATES the loaded model (E1-E29) and exposes
         the warnings via `lblSpec.helpers.validate(root)`.

    The .ts factory does NOT build the model. The model
    is the GLB; the .ts is the wrapper.

    PART 44 is style-agnostic. The architecture applies
    to low-poly, mid-poly, high-poly, smooth-shaded,
    stylized, photoreal, voxel, hand-painted, toon / cel,
    retro-PSX, hologram, wireframe, and any combination.

  44.1  THREE.JS PERFORMANCE: three-mesh-bvh
  -----------------------------------------

    The `three-mesh-bvh` library (already in the
    import map from v0.6) provides BVH-accelerated
    raycasting, culling, and CSG. The renderer
    currently doesn't USE it — the import-map entry
    was added for future use. PART 44 ADDS the
    helper that uses it.

  44.1.1  BVH-accelerated raycasting (picking, collision).

    Without BVH, Three.js's `Raycaster.intersectObject`
    walks every triangle in the geometry to find
    intersections. For a 35k-triangle hero character
    (PART 42.5), this is ~0.5ms per ray — fine for
    60 fps mouse picking (one ray per frame) but
    too slow for collision queries that need N
    rays per frame (N particles, N physics bodies).

    With BVH, the same `Raycaster.intersectObject`
    call uses a pre-computed bounding-volume
    hierarchy to skip whole subtrees. The result is
    ~0.01ms per ray — 50x faster. The trade-off: the
    BVH must be PRE-COMPUTED before the first ray
    query, and it costs ~5MB of memory for a 35k-
    triangle character.

    The `lblSpec.helpers.createBVHIndex(root, opts)`
    helper (44.10) walks the loaded model, finds
    every Mesh, and attaches a `MeshBVH` to the
    geometry's `boundsTree` field. The raycaster
    then auto-uses the BVH when present (the
    three-mesh-bvh library extends the default
    `Raycaster` behavior).

  44.1.2  BVH-accelerated culling.

    BVH also accelerates FRUSTUM CULLING. Without
    BVH, Three.js's frustum-cull pass uses the
    mesh's `boundingSphere` (a single sphere test);
    with BVH, the cull pass uses the pre-computed
    hierarchy to skip whole subtrees that are
    clearly outside the frustum. For a scene with
    1000+ meshes (PART 41.3.1's scene floor), the
    cull pass drops from ~3ms to ~0.3ms.

    The `createBVHIndex` helper enables BOTH
    raycasting AND culling.

  44.1.3  BVH-accelerated CSG (with three-bvh-csg).

    The renderer already imports `three-bvh-csg`
    (from v6.5) for CSG booleans. The BVH integration
    is automatic: the CSG brushes use BVH internally
    for triangle-triangle intersection. PART 44
    doesn't add new code here — the existing CSG
    support from PART 10 is BVH-accelerated by
    virtue of using `three-bvh-csg`.

    The `createBVHIndex` helper's return value
    includes a `csgReady: true` flag so the CSG
    code can confirm the BVH is in place.

  44.2  LOOK-DEV LIGHTS PER MODEL
  -------------------------------

    The renderer currently has a global lighting
    setup (the renderer's `lblSpec` exports a
    `setShadingMode` function for changing the
    shading style — PART 33.1[4]). The brief asks
    for a PER-MODEL look-dev light setup so each
    model can preview with its own lighting.

  44.2.1  The 3-light canonical setup.

    A look-dev rig is a 3-light setup:
      - KEY light: bright, warm, casts shadows. From
        upper-right.
      - FILL light: soft, cool, no shadow. From
        lower-left.
      - RIM light: bright, neutral, no shadow. From
        behind the model.

    The 3 lights give the model a clear sense of
    form without harsh shadows. The intensity
    ratio (key:fill:rim) is 1.0 : 0.4 : 0.6 by
    default.

  44.2.2  HDRI environment map.

    The fill light is often REPLACED by an HDRI
    environment map. The renderer's existing
    `RoomEnvironment` (imported in the module
    script) is the canonical studio-HDRI fallback.
    A model that wants a different environment
    (e.g. an outdoor scene) can supply a custom
    HDRI URL on `meta.environmentMap`.

  44.2.3  Per-model look-dev override.

    The factory publishes the look-dev setup on
    `userData.sculptRuntime.lookDevLights`:
      {
        key: { color, intensity, position, castShadow },
        fill: { color, intensity, position, castShadow },
        rim: { color, intensity, position, castShadow },
        environmentMap: 'studio' | 'outdoor' | 'night' | null,
        enabled: true
      }
    The renderer's runtime either USES the per-
    model setup (when enabled) or falls back to
    the global lights. The renderer's E29 look-
    dev lights validator (44.9) checks that the
    setup is well-formed when present.

  44.3  MATERIAL HIERARCHY (EXTENDING PART 41.6)
  --------------------------------------------

    PART 41.6.1 added 4 material families (plate,
    cloth, leather, stone). The brief asks for 6
    families — add bone + magic. The full
    hierarchy:

      1. skin    — the character's body (face, hands,
                   exposed skin). PBR with subsurface
                   tint.
      2. cloth   — cape, cloak, undershirt, sash.
                   PBR with high roughness.
      3. leather — belt, pouches, boots, gloves.
                   PBR with anisotropy.
      4. metal   — armor plates, weapon receiver,
                   buckles, rivets. PBR with high
                   metalness.
      5. bone    — exposed bone, fangs, claws,
                   horns. PBR with high roughness
                   and a yellowed tint.
      6. magic   — runes, glow effects, energy
                   fields. PBR with emissive.

    PART 44 extends the PART 41.6 `materialVariants`
    helper with the 2 new families. The 6 default
    families now have 4 variants each = 24 base
    materials (up from 16). The 3 dedicated
    secondary materials (scratch / damage /
    engrave) stay.

  44.3.1  The 6 default material families.

    Each family ships with 4 variants. The full
    table is in `lblSpec.helpers.materialVariants`
    (44.10) — the table is the same as PART 41.6.1
    with the bone + magic families added:

      bone:      { bone, boneDark, boneLight, boneWorn }
      magic:     { magic, magicDark, magicLight, magicStrong }

    The 4 default colors per family are:
      - bone: ivory (0xe8dcc0), brown (0x9c7a5a),
        cream (0xf2e8d8), weathered (0x7a6a55).
      - magic: cyan (0x4dd2ff), dark cyan (0x2a8fb3),
        light cyan (0x80e6ff), bright cyan (0x00b8e6).

  44.3.2  Material assignment order.

    The factory assigns materials in a specific
    order to ensure Z-ORDER + RENDER-ORDER are
    correct:

      1. SKIN first (the body underneath).
      2. CLOTH second (cape, undershirt).
      3. LEATHER third (belt, pouches).
      4. METAL fourth (armor, weapons).
      5. BONE fifth (claws, horns).
      6. MAGIC sixth (the glow effects, last so
         they render on top with the highest
         renderOrder).

    The factory's `setStyle(newStyle)` function
    (PART 40.5.3) re-applies the materials in
    this order when the style changes.

  44.3.3  Per-region material override.

    A model can override the family-level variant
    on a per-region basis. The factory publishes
    the overrides on `userData.sculptRuntime
    .materialOverrides`:
      {
        'leftPauldron': 'plateDark',
        'rightPauldron': 'plateDark',
        'cape': 'clothFaded',
        'rune': 'magicStrong'
      }
    The renderer's runtime respects the override
    when present, falling back to the family
    default otherwise.

  44.4  FITTED CLOTHING / ARMOR LAYERS
  ----------------------------------

    PART 41.4.1 added overlapping boxes for
    layered armor; PART 44 EXTENDS the pattern
    with the FIT-OVER-BODY pattern for fitted
    clothing (capes, tabards, vambraces, greaves).

  44.4.1  The fit-over-body pattern.

    A fitted clothing piece (a cape, a vambrace,
    a greave) is NOT a stand-alone box. It is a
    LAYER on top of the body part it covers. The
    canonical pattern:

      1. Start with the body part (the upper arm
         cylinder, the torso tapered box, the
         shoulder sphere).
      2. Compute the clothing's transform: same
         position, slight outward offset (0.005-
         0.02 units) along the body part's surface
         normal, slight scale-up (1.01-1.05x).
      3. Build the clothing's geometry: a SLIGHTLY
         LARGER, SLIGHTLY OFFSETTED version of
         the body part, with edge loops at the
         joint boundaries (PART 38.12).
      4. The clothing has its OWN material (from
         the cloth / leather / metal family per
         44.3) and its OWN skin weights (painted
         to follow the body part's skeleton).

    The key insight: a fitted clothing piece's
    skeleton MIRRORS the body part's skeleton. A
    vambrace's skeleton is the upper arm's
    skeleton + the elbow joint + the wrist joint;
    the vambrace's skin weights are painted so
    each vertex follows the closest underlying
    bone.

  44.4.2  Edge loops for clothing.

    The clothing needs edge loops at the JOINT
    BOUNDARIES where it meets the next body part
    (e.g. the vambrace's lower edge at the wrist
    needs an edge loop that follows the wrist
    joint's rotation). The edge loop count
    matches the body part's joint-edge-loop
    count (PART 38.7 — 3 loops at the elbow, 3-4
    at the wrist, etc.).

  44.4.3  Clothing-specific deformation.

    When the body part bends (the elbow flexes),
    the clothing follows the bend. The
    deformation is HANDLED by the skin weights
    (PART 38.12) — the clothing's vertices are
    weighted to the elbow joint, so the
    flex naturally deforms the clothing. For
    high-stress poses (extreme elbow flexion),
    the clothing may need a CORRECTIVE BLEND
    SHAPE (PART 38.13's procedural corrective
    shapes) to prevent poke-through.

  44.5  ANIMATION CLIP BAKING FOR HERO CHARACTERS
  ---------------------------------------------

    PART 38.13 enumerated 8 animation quality
    checks (idle / walk / attack / hit minimum
    set + extras). PART 42.4.2 listed 30+ blend
    shapes for facial expression. PART 44
    EXTENDS the animation set with the
    IMPLEMENTATION PATTERN for baking the 4
    canonical animation clips.

  44.5.1  The 4 canonical clips.

    A hero character ships with 4 animation
    clips:
      - IDLE: a 60-120 frame loop where the
        character breathes, sways, and looks
        around. Loopable.
      - WALK: a 30-60 frame cycle (one step every
        30 frames) where the character walks in
        place. Loopable.
      - ATTACK: a 20-40 frame one-shot where the
        character swings a weapon. Plays once,
        returns to idle.
      - HIT: a 10-20 frame one-shot where the
        character reacts to damage. Plays once,
        returns to idle.

    The factory publishes the clip set on
    `userData.sculptRuntime.animations`:
      {
        idle: AnimationClip { name: 'idle', duration: 2.0, loop: true },
        walk: AnimationClip { name: 'walk', duration: 1.0, loop: true },
        attack: AnimationClip { name: 'attack', duration: 0.7, loop: false },
        hit: AnimationClip { name: 'hit', duration: 0.4, loop: false }
      }
    The renderer's `lblSpec.triggerFsmState` (PART
    33.1[5]) uses the clip set to wire the FSM.

  44.5.2  Bake from mocap or hand-key.

    The 4 clips can be baked from mocap data
    (BVH / FBX import) or hand-keyed in Blender /
    Maya. PART 44 doesn't specify a source — the
    factory declares the source on
    `meta.animationSource: 'mocap' | 'hand-key' |
    'procedural'`. The renderer's runtime
    respects the source for the BVH-index
    optimization (mocap clips are usually
    smoother and benefit more from BVH
    raycasting).

  44.5.3  Multi-view validation per clip.

    The PART 44.6 multi-view validation renders
    run for EACH of the 4 canonical clips, not
    just the rest pose. The factory publishes
    the per-clip render set on
    `meta.validationClips: ['idle', 'walk',
    'attack', 'hit']` so the renderer knows
    which clips to render for validation.

  44.6  MULTI-VIEW VALIDATION RENDERS
  ----------------------------------

    PART 39.4.1 introduced the 256x256 black-on-
    white silhouette test from the front view;
    PART 43.1.2 introduced view-by-view iterative
    refinement with N camera views. PART 44
    EXTENDS the multi-view pattern with the
    18-RENDER GRID.

  44.6.1  6 canonical angles.

    The 6 canonical camera angles for multi-view
    validation (from PART 40.1.3 / 41.5.1):
      - FRONT (0° azimuth, 0° elevation)
      - 3/4-FRONT (45° azimuth, 0° elevation)
      - SIDE (90° azimuth, 0° elevation)
      - 3/4-BACK (135° azimuth, 0° elevation)
      - BACK (180° azimuth, 0° elevation)
      - TOP (0° azimuth, 90° elevation)

  44.6.2  3 canonical poses.

    The 3 canonical POSES for multi-view
    validation (from PART 38.13 / 42.4):
      - IDLE: the rest pose (T-pose or A-pose).
      - WALK: the walk-cycle frame at 50% (the
        contact pose).
      - ATTACK: the attack frame at 50% (the
        wind-up + strike transition).

  44.6.3  The 18-render grid.

    The 18-RENDER GRID is 6 angles × 3 poses =
    18 silhouette renders per model. The
    factory publishes the grid on
    `userData.sculptRuntime.validationGrid`:
      {
        angles: ['front', '3/4-front', 'side',
                  '3/4-back', 'back', 'top'],
        poses: ['idle', 'walk', 'attack'],
        renders: [ { angle, pose, imageDataUrl,
                     silhouetteIoU: 0.85 }, ... ]
      }
    The IoU (Intersection over Union) vs the
    reference image is computed for each render;
    a model with < 0.7 IoU on any render
    surfaces a warning in the renderer's
    runtime.

    The renderer's E28 BVH index validator
    (44.9) does NOT enforce this — it's an
    authoring-time audit. The runtime E28 only
    checks that the BVH index is in place; the
    18-render grid is for the AI's authoring
    loop.

  44.7  THE TYPESCRIPT VIEWER ADAPTER
  ----------------------------------

    PART 38.14 established the GLB-first
    architecture: the .ts factory becomes the
    viewer adapter. PART 44 EXTENDS the adapter
    pattern with the 5 specific tasks the brief
    calls out.

  44.7.1  Load GLB.

    The adapter uses `GLTFLoader` from
    `three/addons/loaders/GLTFLoader.js` to load
    the GLB. The adapter's `create*Model()`
    function does NOT build the model — it
    dispatches the GLB load and returns a Promise:

      export async function create*Model() {
        const loader = new GLTFLoader();
        const gltf = await loader.loadAsync(
          'path/to/model.glb'
        );
        return gltf.scene;  // The loaded Group.
      }

  44.7.2  Expose sockets.

    The adapter extracts the 4 canonical Socket_*
    attachment points from the GLB's
    userData.sculptRuntime.sockets (set by the
    GLB exporter) and exposes them on the Group:

      root.userData.sockets = {
        'Socket_Weapon_Primary': socketMesh,
        'Socket_Weapon_Secondary': socketMesh2,
        'Socket_Back': socketMesh3,
        'Socket_Head': socketMesh4
      };

  44.7.3  Wire animation mixer.

    The adapter creates an `AnimationMixer` and
    registers the 4 canonical clips:

      const mixer = new AnimationMixer(root);
      const clips = gltf.animations;
      const actions = {
        idle: mixer.clipAction(
          AnimationClip.findByName(clips, 'idle')
        ),
        walk: mixer.clipAction(...),
        attack: mixer.clipAction(...),
        hit: mixer.clipAction(...)
      };
      root.userData.animationFsm = {
        mixer, actions,
        trigger: (state) => { actions[state].play(); }
      };

  44.7.4  Wire look-dev lights.

    The adapter instantiates the 3-light setup
    (key + fill + rim) from PART 44.2.1 and
    adds them to the scene:

      const lights = lblSpec.helpers.lookDevLights({
        keyIntensity: 1.0,
        fillIntensity: 0.4,
        rimIntensity: 0.6,
        castShadow: true
      });
      lights.forEach(l => root.add(l));

  44.7.5  Wire validation.

    The adapter calls `lblSpec.helpers.validateModel`
    (PART 39.1.2) on the loaded root and logs the
    warnings:

      const v = lblSpec.helpers.validateModel(root, {
        maxGap: 0.02
      });
      if(!v.ok){
        console.warn('[create*Model]',
          v.errors.length, 'issue(s):', v.errors);
      }

    The renderer's E1-E29 validators run on
    every model load (PART 32 / 65 / 38 / 39 /
    40 / 41 / 42 / 43 / 44); the adapter's
    `validateModel` call catches authoring-time
    issues before the runtime does.

  44.8  PRACTICAL CODING RULES (THE MAINTAINER'S HOUSE STYLE)
  ---------------------------------------------------------

    The brief lists 6 PRACTICAL CODING RULES for
    .ts factory code. PART 44 documents them as
    house style — every .ts factory in the
    project should follow them.

  44.8.1  No code on the same line after a `//`
          comment separator.

    BAD:
      // ------- section header
      // -------const COL = { ... };

    GOOD:
      // ------- section header
      // -----------------------------------------------------
      const COL = { ... };

    This is the same rule as PART 39.1.1; PART
    44 reinforces it for the new factories
    written under the GLB-first architecture.

  44.8.2  Always leave a blank line after section
          header comments.

    BAD:
      // -----------------------------------------------------
      const COL = { ... };
      const M = { ... };
      // -----------------------------------------------------
      const create = () => { ... };

    GOOD:
      // -----------------------------------------------------
      const COL = { ... };
      const M = { ... };

      // -----------------------------------------------------
      const create = () => { ... };

  44.8.3  Prefer the correct geometry primitive.

    This is the same rule as PART 39.3 — use
    ExtrudeGeometry + CatmullRomCurve3 for
    curved surfaces, LatheGeometry for round
    profiles, InstancedMesh for repeated
    detail, BufferGeometry merge for one-off
    detail. The PART 44 reinforcement is that
    the new `curvedPart` helper (PART 39.8) is
    the canonical answer for "stop stacking
    boxes to fake curves".

  44.8.4  Drive everything from a single config
          object at the top of the file.

    Every .ts factory has a single `*_CONFIG`
    object at the top of the file that drives
    ALL dimensions, colors, curve points,
    recoil numbers. The factory's `create*Model()`
    function reads from `*_CONFIG` and passes
    the relevant slice to each sub-factory. The
    same pattern as PART 39.5.2 + 40.4.2 +
    42.0.

  44.8.5  Add runtime tweakable parameters
          (Tweakpane / Leva style) and a boot-
          time model validator.

    The factory includes a Tweakpane (or Leva
    or dat.gui) panel that exposes the most
    important `*_CONFIG` values as sliders /
    dropdowns. The panel is for the AI's
    authoring-time iteration (PART 39.2.1) and
    is NOT shipped in the final emission. The
    factory ALSO calls `validateModel(root)`
    at the end of `create*Model()` (PART
    39.1.2) and logs warnings.

  44.8.6  Include automatic multi-view validation
          renders (front, side, back, three-
          quarter, idle, walk, attack poses).

    The factory's authoring-time harness
    renders the 18-render grid (PART 44.6.3)
    and writes the renders to disk + a
    `meta.validationGrid` JSON file. The
    harness is for the AI's authoring loop and
    is NOT shipped in the final emission.

  44.9  RENDERER-SIDE RUNTIME CONTRACT
  ------------------------------------

    PART 44 adds 2 new non-blocking validators to
    the existing 27:

      - E28 — BVH index presence (PART 44.1.1).
        If the loaded model declares
        `userData.sculptRuntime.bvhIndex` (or
        `userData.useBVH: true`), the renderer
        checks that the BVH index is in place
        (i.e. every Mesh's geometry has a
        `boundsTree` field, OR the
        `MeshBVH` accelerator is attached). A
        model that says "use BVH" but doesn't
        have the index in place is a warning.
        Non-blocking; the model still loads.

      - E29 — Look-dev lights present (PART
        44.2). If the model declares
        `userData.sculptRuntime.lookDevLights`,
        the renderer checks that the structure
        is well-formed (key + fill + rim, all
        with `intensity` and `position`). A
        model with malformed look-dev lights
        surfaces a warning. Non-blocking.

    All 2 are non-blocking; the model still
    loads, the user sees a toast + a red spec
    chip. They mirror the E1-E27 pattern and
    integrate into the existing
    `validate()` / `applyToUI()` / `revalidate()`
    pipeline without any caller-side changes.

    What PART 44 does NOT change in the renderer
    runtime:

      - No change to E1-E27 (they stay exactly
        as they were in v1.20 / v8.12).
      - No change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX /
        DAE / USDZ / .ts export pipeline.
      - No change to the import map (the
        three-mesh-bvh entry was already in
        place from v0.6).
      - No change to the loader.
      - No change to the file-picker UI.
      - No change to the scene tree or the Rig /
        Tris / Sockets buttons.
      - No new mandatory field on any existing
        userData namespace — the new fields from
        44.2.3 `lookDevLights`, 44.5.1
        `animations`, 44.5.2 `animationSource`,
        44.5.3 `validationClips`, 44.6.3
        `validationGrid`, 44.1.1 `bvhIndex` /
        `useBVH` are ALL OPTIONAL.
      - No new dependency.

  44.10 CANONICAL HELPERS
  ----------------------

    PART 44 adds 2 new canonical helpers to the
    existing `__threeTriHelpers` bundle (PART
    35 + PART 39 + PART 40 + PART 41 + PART 42 +
    PART 43). The .ts factory can call any of
    them via `window.lblSpec.helpers.<name>`
    without copying the function body.

    - `createBVHIndex(root, opts)`
        PART 44.1 / Appendix A. Build a
        BVH-accelerated raycast structure for
        a loaded model. The helper:
          - Walks the model's mesh tree.
          - For each Mesh, imports
            `MeshBVH` from `three-mesh-bvh`
            (already in the import map) and
            attaches a `MeshBVH` to the
            geometry's `boundsTree` field.
          - Skips Mesh with `userData
            .skipBVH: true` (the AI can opt
            out per-mesh for tiny detail that
            doesn't need BVH acceleration).
          - Returns a summary:
            `{ totalMeshes, indexedMeshes,
            skippedMeshes, memoryEstimateBytes,
            csgReady: true }`.

        The `Raycaster.intersectObject` call
        auto-uses the BVH when the geometry has
        a `boundsTree` (three-mesh-bvh extends
        the default behavior). The renderer's
        E28 BVH index validator uses the same
        helper.

    - `lookDevLights(opts)`
        PART 44.2 / Appendix B. Return the
        canonical 3-light setup (key + fill +
        rim) for a model. The helper:
          - Creates a `DirectionalLight` for
            the key (warm color, casts shadow,
            from upper-right).
          - Creates a `DirectionalLight` for
            the fill (cool color, no shadow,
            from lower-left).
          - Creates a `DirectionalLight` for
            the rim (neutral color, no shadow,
            from behind).
          - Returns an array of 3 lights (or
            a Group containing the 3 lights)
            that the factory adds to the
            scene.

        The optional `opts.environmentMap`
        ('studio' | 'outdoor' | 'night' | null)
        adds a `RoomEnvironment` (or a custom
        HDRI) as a 4th element. The renderer's
        E29 look-dev lights validator uses the
        same helper.

    These are pure additions to the existing
    `__threeTriHelpers` bundle — the existing
    5 PART 35 helpers, the 3 PART 39 helpers,
    the 3 PART 40 helpers, the 2 PART 41
    helpers, the 2 PART 42 helpers, and the 2
    PART 43 helpers stay untouched. The
    `materialVariants` helper from PART 41 is
    EXTENDED (in 44.3) with the 2 new families
    (bone + magic) — the existing 4 families
    stay exactly as they were.

  WHAT PART 44 DOES NOT INTRODUCE
  --------------------------------

    - No new mandatory field on any existing
      userData namespace (the new fields in
      44.1.1 `bvhIndex` / `useBVH`, 44.2.3
      `lookDevLights`, 44.3.3
      `materialOverrides`, 44.4 `*` (the
      fit-over-body pattern is a runtime
      behavior, not a meta field), 44.5.1
      `animations`, 44.5.2 `animationSource`,
      44.5.3 `validationClips`, 44.6.3
      `validationGrid` are ALL OPTIONAL; the
      current renderer ignores unknown keys
      safely)
    - No new export path (the existing GLB /
      OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE / USDZ / .ts paths
      are unchanged)
    - No new dependency (the three-mesh-bvh
      import-map entry was already in place
      from v0.6; the new helper just uses it;
      the lookDevLights helper uses only
      Three.js core APIs that are already
      imported; no import-map change)
    - No new validator that blocks the model
      from loading (E28, E29 are non-blocking,
      like E1-E27 before them)
    - No change to the existing PART 1-43
      rules (PART 44 reinforces them — see the
      44.0 + 44.7 + 44.8 cross-references)
    - No change to the existing E1-E27
      validators (they stay exactly as they
      were in v1.20 / v8.12)
    - No change to PART 38.22's style
      agnosticism (PART 44 is also style-
      agnostic)
    - No change to PART 38.14's GLB-first
      architecture (PART 44 extends it with
      the 5-task viewer adapter pattern)
    - No change to PART 39.5's factory +
      options pattern (PART 44 reinforces it
      as 44.8.4 + 44.8.5)
    - No change to PART 41.6's 4 default
      material families (PART 44.3 extends
      them with bone + magic; the existing 4
      families stay exactly as they were)
    - No change to the project's "Low Poly 3D"
      name

END OF PART 44 — v1.21 / v8.13 ADDITIONS: PROFESSIONAL PIPELINE
(GLB-FIRST + BVH + LOOK-DEV LIGHTS + CODING RULES)
Ship the GLB; the .ts factory is the viewer adapter. 5-task adapter
pattern: load GLB (44.7.1) + expose sockets (44.7.2) + wire animation
mixer (44.7.3) + wire look-dev lights (44.7.4) + wire validation
(44.7.5). 3-tier performance: BVH-accelerated raycasting (44.1.1) for
picking/collision, BVH-accelerated culling (44.1.2) for large scenes,
BVH-accelerated CSG (44.1.3) for the existing PART 10 boolean ops
(uses the already-imported three-bvh-csg). 6-family material
hierarchy: skin + cloth + leather + metal (existing from PART 41.6)
+ bone + magic (new in 44.3), each with 4 variants = 24 base
materials. The material assignment order: skin → cloth → leather →
metal → bone → magic (44.3.2) so the render-order is correct. Fitted
clothing/armor layers (44.4): fit-over-body pattern with skin weights
that mirror the body part's skeleton, edge loops at joint boundaries,
corrective blend shapes for extreme poses. Animation clip baking
(44.5): 4 canonical clips (idle 2s loop, walk 1s loop, attack 0.7s
one-shot, hit 0.4s one-shot) with `meta.animationSource: 'mocap' |
'hand-key' | 'procedural'`. Multi-view validation renders (44.6): 6
canonical angles × 3 canonical poses = 18-render grid, with IoU
(Intersection over Union) vs reference image per render; a model
with <0.7 IoU on any render surfaces a warning. The 6 practical
coding rules (44.8) — no code on the same line after a `//`
comment separator, blank line after section header comments, prefer
the correct geometry primitive, drive everything from a single
config object at the top of the file, add runtime tweakable
parameters (Tweakpane / Leva style) + a boot-time model validator,
include automatic multi-view validation renders. Adds E28 BVH
index presence (checks `userData.sculptRuntime.bvhIndex`) and E29
look-dev lights present (checks the key + fill + rim structure) to
the existing 27 non-blocking validators. Re-exports `createBVHIndex
(root, opts)` and `lookDevLights(opts)` on `window.lblSpec.helpers`
alongside the PART 35 + PART 39 + PART 40 + PART 41 + PART 42 +
PART 43 bundle. The `materialVariants` helper from PART 41 is
EXTENDED (44.3) with the 2 new families (bone + magic); the existing
4 families stay exactly as they were. No new dependency (the
three-mesh-bvh import-map entry was already in place from v0.6; the
new helpers use it). No new export path, no new mandatory field,
no removed / weakened rule. Style-agnostic, applies to every .ts
factory + JSON/JS blueprint, every subject, every style.
