================================================================================
PART 45 — v1.22 / v8.14 ADDITIONS: CS2 PBR MATERIAL PROFILE (OPT-IN)
================================================================================

  The JS path is the `eval()`-compiled sibling of the JSON
  path; it accepts the same `meta.cs2Finish` /
  `meta.cs2Wear` keys and runs the same `cs2PbrProfile` +
  `applyWear` helper pipeline. PART 45's opt-in capability,
  9 finishes, 5-tier wear system, 8 MAT_DB presets, 4 new
  MeshPhysicalMaterial properties, 5 canonical helpers,
  and 1 new validator E30 all apply unchanged. The 45.0.2
  "extra capability, not a rule" framing is identical on
  all 4 paths (TS prompt + TS image + JSON/JS prompt +
  JSON/JS image).

OVERVIEW
--------

  PART 45 layers onto PARTs 1-44 as an OPT-IN capability for
  the .ts factory and JSON/JS blueprint authors. The source is
  the maintainer's "3D Technical Reference — CS2 to Three.js
  Mapping" research notebook (id `30ec3dba-5a20-4980-8886-
  ce89b506b634`, July 2026), which catalogues how
  Counter-Strike 2 weapon finishes (the 8 families CS2 itself
  uses, plus 1 bonus finish the notebook treats separately)
  map onto Three.js MeshPhysicalMaterial, and how CS2's
  per-item float value drives a wear parameter that blends
  the PBR properties.

  The single most important rule:

    CS2 PBR is an EXTRA CAPABILITY, not a rule. The .ts
    factory uses it WHEN the subject calls for it (knives,
    gun hardware, polished/brushed metal, CS2-style finish
    requests) and SKIPS it otherwise. No existing rule is
    changed; no existing material default is changed; no
    model that doesn't opt in loads any differently.

  45.0.1  EXTENDS, not replaces. The .ts factory's material
          contract from PART 3 (color / metalness / roughness /
          clearcoat / sheen / anisotropy / transmission) is
          the BASE. The new `cs2PbrProfile(finish, opts)`
          helper from 45.10.1 returns a property bundle the
          factory spreads ON TOP of the PART 3 base; the
          renderer's `buildMaterial` already accepts all of
          these as direct per-object overrides (PART 3), so
          the override mechanism is unchanged. The only new
          bits are `iridescence` / `iridescenceIOR` /
          `iridescenceThicknessRange` / `specularIntensity` /
          `specularColor` (45.5) which the renderer now
          wires through alongside the existing `clearcoat` /
          `sheen` / `anisotropy` / `transmission` plumbing.

  45.0.2  WHEN TO OPT IN. The .ts factory requests
          `cs2Finish` ONLY when the subject matches one of
          the 5 use cases in 45.8 (knives, guns, metal
          props, CS2 displays, or an explicit user
          "CS2-style" / "anodized" / "case hardened" /
          "Doppler" / "Fade" / "Damascus" / "patina"
          request). For every other subject — fabric,
          leather, skin, wood, character clothing, food,
          plants, vehicles (full vehicles, not metal
          parts), fantasy / magic, organic shapes, etc. —
          the .ts factory should NOT set `cs2Finish` and
          the model loads on the default PART 3 + PART 41 +
          PART 44 pipeline. There is no default
          `cs2Finish`; the field is purely opt-in.

  45.0.3  STYLE-AGNOSTIC IN SCOPE. PART 45 applies
          regardless of `meta.style` — a low-poly CS2 knife
          is a valid subject for `cs2Finish:
          'casehardened'` (just with a lower triangle budget
          than a mid-poly one), and a photoreal CS2 rifle is
          equally valid. The CS2 finishes are CONTENT, not
          STYLE.

  45.0.4  LAYERING ON THE EXISTING STACK. PART 45 layers on
          top of, never replaces:
            - PART 3  (material contract)         — TS path
            - PART 3 advanced PBR (clearcoat/sheen/
              anisotropy/transmission)             — TS path
            - PART 7  #7 PBR Extensions            — JSON/JS
            - PART 41.6 (materialVariants family) — both
            - PART 44.3 (6-family material)        — both
            - PART 44  (professional pipeline)     — both
          The .ts factory that already uses PART 44.3's
          `materialVariants` gets the new `cs2-finish`
          family added on top (45.10.3). The `cs2Finish`
          field is a per-material hint, not a wholesale
          material-class switch; a "skin" CS2 knife with
          `cs2Finish: 'anodizedblue'` would be a
          contradiction (skin isn't metal), and E30 surfaces
          a warning if the resolved material's metalness /
          base material doesn't match the finish's expected
          substrate.

    45.0.5  PATH DIFFERENCE. The JS path is a syntactic
          variant of the JSON path — `meta.cs2Finish:
          'casehardened'` works in both. The JS path doesn't
          add any PART-45-specific behavior; it just makes
          the JSON syntax friendlier for the user (object
          literals instead of strict JSON, comments allowed).
          Everything else mirrors the JSON path exactly.


45.1 — PBR PROPERTY MAP
-----------------------

  The MeshPhysicalMaterial properties PART 45 can drive, in
  priority order. The first 6 are already covered by PART 3
  (TS) and PART 7 (JSON/JS); the .ts factory's existing
  `buildMaterial` path. The last 7 are NEW in v1.22 / v8.14
  and the renderer's `buildMaterial` now wires them through
  (45.5) so the .ts factory / JSON blueprint can set them
  on any object the same way it sets `clearcoat` or
  `transmission`.

  Property                       Type        Range        Effect
  -----------------------------  ----------  -----------  -----------
  color                          Color       —            Base albedo
  metalness                      Float       0.0–1.0      0=dielectric, 1=metal
  roughness                      Float       0.0–1.0      0=mirror, 1=matte
  envMapIntensity                Float       0.0–∞        Reflection strength
  clearcoat                      Float       0.0–1.0      Lacquer/varnish layer
  clearcoatRoughness             Float       0.0–1.0      Clearcoat roughness
  sheen                          Float       0.0–1.0      Velvet/fabric backscatter
  sheenColor                     Color       —            Sheen tint
  sheenRoughness                 Float       0.0–1.0      Sheen roughness
  anisotropy                     Float       0.0–1.0      Stretched highlight
  anisotropyRotation             Float       0–2π         Direction (radians)
  transmission                   Float       0.0–1.0      Light transmission
  thickness                      Float       0.0–∞        Absorption thickness
  ior                            Float       1.0–2.33     Refraction index
  --- NEW in v1.22 / v8.14 -----------------------------------
  iridescence                    Float       0.0–1.0      Thin-film interference
  iridescenceIOR                 Float       1.0–3.0      Iridescent layer IOR
  iridescenceThicknessRange      [min,max]   nm (100–800) Film thickness range
  specularIntensity              Float       0.0–1.0      F0 knob (dielectric)
  specularColor                  Color       —            F0 tint (dielectric)
  attenuationColor               Color       —            Transmission absorption
  attenuationDistance            Float       0.0–∞        Absorption distance

  All 7 "NEW" properties are real MeshPhysicalMaterial /
  MeshStandardMaterial properties that have shipped in
  Three.js for years. The renderer's v1.22 / v8.14 build
  just wires them through `buildMaterial` (45.5) so a .ts
  factory / JSON blueprint can request them via the same
  `obj.<property>: <value>` override syntax the existing
  PART 3 / PART 7 contract already uses for `clearcoat` /
  `transmission` / `sheen` / `anisotropy`. No new
  dependency; no new import; no new module path.

  iridescence / iridescenceIOR / iridescenceThicknessRange
  work ONLY on MeshPhysicalMaterial. The renderer's
  existing `wantsPhysical` switch (the same one that
  already flips on for `clearcoat` / `transmission` /
  `sheen` / `anisotropy`) now ALSO flips on when ANY of
  `iridescence`, `iridescenceIOR`, or
  `iridescenceThicknessRange` is set on the object (45.5.1).

  specularIntensity / specularColor work on BOTH
  MeshStandardMaterial and MeshPhysicalMaterial (they're F0
  knobs for dielectrics). The renderer wires them through
  on either class (45.5.2).

  attenuationColor / attenuationDistance only have a visual
  effect when `transmission > 0`. The renderer wires them
  through on MeshPhysicalMaterial only (45.5.3).


45.2 — CS2 FINISH-STYLE → PBR PROFILE
-------------------------------------

  The 9 CS2 finish styles from the maintainer's notebook,
  each pinned to a property tuple. The .ts factory sets
  `cs2Finish: '<name>'` and the renderer resolves the
  profile via the new `cs2PbrProfile(name, opts)` helper
  (45.10.1).

  ── 45.2.1  Anodized ─────────────────────────────────────
    Substrate: full metal
    metalness:        1.0
    roughness:        0.05–0.15
    clearcoat:        0.3–0.5
    clearcoatRoughness:0.05
    iridescence:      0.0–0.2     (subtle angle-shift)
    color:            tinted (blue / gold / red / purple /
                      etc.)
    envMapIntensity:  1.3
    Wear behavior:    Color coating peels from edges,
                      revealing the bare metal underneath.
                      The notebook's examples: Fade, Blue
                      Steel, Case Hardened (anodized
                      variant).
    CS2 examples:     Fade, Blue Steel, Anodized Air,
                      Anodized Navy.

  ── 45.2.2  Anodized Multicolored ─────────────────────────
    Substrate: full metal
    metalness:        1.0
    roughness:        0.05–0.10
    clearcoat:        0.0–0.1
    iridescence:      0.3–0.5     (strong multi-color shift)
    iridescenceIOR:   1.5–2.0     (higher IOR = more colors)
    iridescenceThicknessRange: [100, 500]
    color:            base hue (e.g. 0xff6600 for ruby
                      Fade)
    envMapIntensity:  1.5
    Wear behavior:    Gloss fades first, THEN the color
                      gradient shifts with wear. The
                      notebook's note: the color shift is
                      the WHOLE POINT — wear reveals
                      different iridescent colors at
                      different angles, so the post-wear
                      look isn't "faded", it's "different
                      palette".
    CS2 examples:     Doppler (all phases — Ruby / Sapphire
                      / Black Pearl / Phase 1–4 / Emerald),
                      Fade 100% (Fire / Ice / Acid / Storm
                      / etc.).

  ── 45.2.3  Custom Paint Job ──────────────────────────────
    Substrate: non-metallic paint over a metal base
    metalness:        0.0
    roughness:        0.3–0.5
    clearcoat:        0.2–0.4
    clearcoatRoughness:0.1–0.2
    color / map:      custom paint texture (the .ts factory
                      passes the procedural pattern via
                      `texturePattern` or `map` from PART 5)
    envMapIntensity:  1.0
    Wear behavior:    Paint scratches from edges and high-
                      friction areas, revealing bare metal.
                      The exposed-metal look needs the wear
                      pipeline (45.3) to drop `metalness`
                      toward 1.0 in the worn region.
    CS2 examples:     Slaughter, Crimson Web, Asiimov,
                      Hyper Beast, Neon Rider, The Empress.

  ── 45.2.4  Gunsmith ──────────────────────────────────────
    Substrate: partly painted, partly chemically treated
                metal
    metalness:        0.7–0.9
    roughness:        0.2–0.4
    clearcoat:        0.0–0.1
    color:            dark olive / gunmetal (e.g. 0x333344)
    envMapIntensity:  1.0
    Wear behavior:    Combined paint fading + metal
                      exposure, with a chemical-treatment
                      look. The notebook's note: this
                      finish is the HALFWAY POINT between
                      "Custom Paint" and "Patina" — the
                      substrate IS metal but the surface
                      treatment is non-uniform.
    CS2 examples:     Night, Corticera, Guardian,
                      Snakebite.

  ── 45.2.5  Hydrographic ──────────────────────────────────
    Substrate: film transfer over a metal base
    metalness:        0.0     (the FILM is non-metallic;
                              the base metal shows only
                              where the film is worn off)
    roughness:        0.5–0.7
    clearcoat:        0.1–0.2
    color / map:      camo / pattern texture
    envMapIntensity:  1.0
    Wear behavior:    Large patches peel off (NOT edge
                      wear), exposing bare metal. The
                      notebook's examples are wildlife /
                      camo patterns.
    CS2 examples:     Boreal Forest, Safari Mesh, Arctic
                      Wolf, Forest Leaves.

  ── 45.2.6  Patina ────────────────────────────────────────
    Substrate: full metal with a surface oxidation layer
    metalness:        0.9–1.0
    roughness:        0.2–0.4
    iridescence:      0.3–0.6     (color-shifting oxidation)
    iridescenceIOR:   1.3–1.5
    color:            blue-grey base (e.g. 0x667788) — the
                      iridescence does the heavy color work
    envMapIntensity:  1.3
    Wear behavior:    Color CHANGES (darkens / shifts)
                      rather than peeling. The notebook's
                      note: the oxidation layer is part of
                      the surface, so it doesn't flake off
                      — it migrates into different patterns.
    CS2 examples:     Case Hardened, Blue Steel (patina
                      variant), Damascus Steel (see
                      45.2.9).

  ── 45.2.7  Solid Color ───────────────────────────────────
    Substrate: opaque non-metallic coating
    metalness:        0.0
    roughness:        0.3–0.5
    clearcoat:        0.2–0.3
    color / map:      solid color (e.g. 0x556633 for
                      olive)
    envMapIntensity:  1.0
    Wear behavior:    Scratches reveal underlying metal at
                      edges; the wear pipeline (45.3) drops
                      `metalness` toward 1.0 in the worn
                      region.
    CS2 examples:     Urban Masked, Jungle Spray, Olive
                      (solid), Sand (solid).

  ── 45.2.8  Sprayed ───────────────────────────────────────
    Substrate: rough non-metallic paint over a metal base
    metalness:        0.0
    roughness:        0.6–0.8
    clearcoat:        0.0
    color / map:      spray pattern (random / gradient)
    envMapIntensity:  0.9
    Wear behavior:    Heavy scratching; the base metal is
                      clearly visible at edges. The
                      roughness ramp is steeper than "Solid
                      Color" (more paint loss per wear
                      unit).
    CS2 examples:     Scorched, Sand Dune, Dragon Lore
                      (spray variant).

  ── 45.2.9  Damascus Steel (BONUS — separate from Patina) ─
    Substrate: forged / folded steel with a layered pattern
    metalness:        1.0
    roughness:        0.25–0.40
    iridescence:      0.2–0.4     (subtle)
    iridescenceIOR:   1.2–1.3
    color:            dark steel base (e.g. 0x4a4a52)
    anisotropy:       0.4–0.7     (the FOLDED-STEEL grain)
    anisotropyRotation: matched to the damascus pattern
                       direction
    envMapIntensity:  1.4
    Wear behavior:    The layered pattern BECOMES MORE
                      VISIBLE as it wears (the dark oxide
                      between layers is harder than the
                      polished layers, so it stays dark
                      while the layers around it scratch
                      light). The notebook calls this the
                      "Damascus wear inversion" — a unique
                      case where wear ENHANCES the visual
                      signature.
    CS2 examples:     Damascus Steel, Tiger Tooth, Lore
                      (Damascus variant).

  All 9 profiles are constants in the new `cs2PbrProfile`
  helper (45.10.1). The .ts factory / JSON blueprint never
  writes the numbers inline; it asks the helper. The
  constant tables live in the helper for the same reason
  PART 41.6's `materialVariants` constants do — one source
  of truth, one place to tune.


45.3 — WEAR SYSTEM BRIDGE
--------------------------

  CS2's float value (0.000–1.000) drives a 5-tier wear
  system that the .ts factory / JSON blueprint translates
  to a single 0-1 `wearAmount` parameter. The renderer
  reads `meta.cs2Wear` (a number 0-1 OR a string 'fn' /
  'mw' / 'ft' / 'ww' / 'bs') and the new `applyWear(mat,
  wearAmount, baseMetal, baseColor)` helper (45.10.2)
  applies the per-region PBR shift.

  ── 45.3.1  Wear tiers ────────────────────────────────────

    Tier           Float range   Visual
    -------------  ------------  ----------------------------
    Factory New    0.00–0.07     Pristine. Almost no wear.
    Minimal Wear   0.07–0.15     Minor edge scratches. Clean.
    Field-Tested   0.15–0.38     Noticeable scratches, edge
                                 fade.
    Well-Worn      0.38–0.45     Significant paint loss.
    Battle-Scarred 0.45–1.00     Heavy damage, deep
                                 scratches, large blemishes.

  ── 45.3.2  Per-skin float clipping ───────────────────────

    CS2 restricts float ranges per skin via
    `wear_remap_min` and `wear_remap_max` in
    `items_game.txt` (e.g. `{"wear_remap_min": 0.00,
    "wear_remap_max": 0.80}` — a skin with remap_max 0.80
    can NEVER reach Battle-Scarred). The .ts factory
    declares its skin's range via `meta.cs2WearRemap =
    {min, max}` and the renderer clamps the requested
    `meta.cs2Wear` to that range before passing it to
    `applyWear`. This is the same behavior CS2 itself
    enforces. The field is OPTIONAL — when absent, the
    renderer assumes the full 0.00–1.00 range.

  ── 45.3.3  PBR parameter shift per wear unit ─────────────

    The wear pipeline mutates 3 properties:

    1.  roughness ramps UP from `roughnessClean` to
        `roughnessWorn` (default 0.3 → 0.7). The clean
        value comes from the finish profile (45.2); the
        worn value is a global default of 0.7 that the
        .ts factory can override via
        `opts.roughnessWorn`.

    2.  metalness ramps UP toward the base metal
        (`baseMetal`, default 1.0) as paint / coating
        wears off. The notebook's note: this only
        matters for finishes whose `metalness` is NOT
        already 1.0 — Anodized / Custom Paint / Hydro
        / Gunsmith / Solid Color / Sprayed. Patina and
        Damascus Steel are already at metalness 0.9+;
        the wear pipeline clamps their metalness to the
        profile default and doesn't try to push higher.

    3.  color DESATURATES from the finish's `baseColor`
        toward the `baseColor * 0.7` (a 30%
        desaturation) as the surface oxidizes / fades.
        Damascus Steel and Patina SKIP this step (the
        worn look is DARKER, not desat'd; the helper
        detects these from the profile and skips the
        desaturation pass).

    The .ts factory that wants a wear-tunable weapon
    calls `applyWear(mat, wearAmount, baseMetal=1.0,
    baseColor=<finish color>)` once after `cs2PbrProfile`
    resolves the clean material. The helper mutates
    `mat.roughness`, `mat.metalness`, and `mat.color` in
    place; the .ts factory doesn't need to rebuild the
    material to slide the wear slider.

  ── 45.3.4  Spatial wear (where on the part) ──────────────

    The notebook's spatial wear glossary is a CONTENT
    GUIDE, not a runtime change. The .ts factory picks
    the wear location from the model's geometry — the
    "exposed core" (45.3.4.5) of a knife blade is the
    tip + spine edge; a gun's "edge wear" is the slide
    rail + magazine well; etc. The renderer doesn't
    try to procedurally pick the wear location — the
    .ts factory either:
      a) applies wear uniformly (one wearAmount for the
         whole material — the simple case, used for
         miniature / display subjects);
      b) splits the part into 2-3 sub-meshes, each
         with its own wearAmount (the realistic case
         for a hero weapon — e.g. blade body at
         wearAmount 0.4, blade edge at wearAmount 0.7,
         handle at 0.15);
      c) authors a `wearMap` (CanvasTexture, red
         channel = wear mask) and the renderer
         multiplies it against the wearAmount (the
         most realistic case — see 45.3.5).

  ── 45.3.5  wearMap / wear mask (optional) ────────────────

    When the .ts factory wants a procedural wear
    pattern (CS2's "battle-scarred" weapons use a noise
    + edge mask combination) it authors a CanvasTexture
    and sets it via the new `mat.wearMap` field. The
    renderer reads the red channel (0 at no-wear, 1 at
    full-wear) and multiplies it against the
    wearAmount. The .ts factory builds the mask with
    `THREE.DataTexture(RGBA Uint8)` — NOT an external
    image file (the no-external-asset rule from PART 5
    is unchanged). The new `cs2WearMaskTexture(size,
    opts)` helper (45.10.4) is a drop-in for this.

    The helper is OPT-IN. When absent, the renderer
    assumes uniform wear across the material. The CS2
    PBR profile still works without a wearMap — it just
    looks the same all over the part, which is the
    right answer for many subjects (small props,
    miniatures, display pieces).


45.4 — MATERIAL PRESET RECIPES (8 new MAT_DB entries)
-----------------------------------------------------

  The renderer's MAT_DB (the lookup table of named material
  presets used by `material: 'brushedsteel'` style strings)
  EXTENDS with 8 new presets in v1.22 / v8.14. The
  existing 30+ presets (skin, eye, glass, paint, chrome,
  rust, etc.) are UNTOUCHED.

  ── 45.4.1  polishedmetal / chrome ───────────────────────
    The existing v6.4 `chrome` entry (`{c:0xcccccc,
    r:0.05, m:0.98}`) is the canonical version;
    `polishedmetal` is the new alias the .ts factory /
    JSON blueprint can also use. envMapIntensity: 1.5
    on the alias for the classic CS2 "polished" look.

  ── 45.4.2  brushedsteel (NEW) ─────────────────────────────
    {c:0x888888, r:0.30, m:1.0, anisotropy: 0.8,
     anisotropyRotation: π/4}
    Satin / brushed finish. The notebook's 0.8
    anisotropy is the sweet spot for "this is clearly
    brushed metal" without going full "this is a
    vinyl record".

  ── 45.4.3  gunmetal (NEW) ────────────────────────────────
    {c:0x3a3a3a, r:0.45, m:1.0, envMapIntensity: 1.2}
    Dark steel / blued finish. The notebook's 0.45
    roughness is the right mid-point between polished
    (0.05) and matte (0.7).

  ── 45.4.4  damascussteel (NEW) ───────────────────────────
    {c:0x4a4a52, r:0.30, m:1.0, anisotropy: 0.5,
     anisotropyRotation: π/4, iridescence: 0.3,
     iridescenceIOR: 1.25, envMapIntensity: 1.4}
    The folded-steel pattern (45.2.9). The factory
    supplies the procedural pattern via a normalMap
    (the .ts factory generates it in a CanvasTexture).

  ── 45.4.5  anodizedblue (NEW) ────────────────────────────
    {c:0x2244aa, r:0.08, m:1.0, clearcoat: 0.4,
     clearcoatRoughness: 0.05, envMapIntensity: 1.3}
    The notebook's Anodized Blue example. The 0.4
    clearcoat is the protective glossy layer that
    distinguishes "anodized" from "plain polished
    metal".

  ── 45.4.6  anodizedred (NEW) ─────────────────────────────
    {c:0xaa2244, r:0.08, m:1.0, clearcoat: 0.4,
     clearcoatRoughness: 0.05, envMapIntensity: 1.3}
    Same profile as anodizedblue, red base.

  ── 45.4.7  anodizedgold (NEW) ────────────────────────────
    {c:0xccaa22, r:0.08, m:1.0, clearcoat: 0.4,
     clearcoatRoughness: 0.05, envMapIntensity: 1.3}
    Same profile as anodizedblue, gold base.

  ── 45.4.8  casehardened (NEW) ────────────────────────────
    {c:0x667788, r:0.25, m:1.0, iridescence: 0.5,
     iridescenceIOR: 1.4, envMapIntensity: 1.3}
    The notebook's "Patina (Case Hardened / Blue
    Gem)" example. The 0.5 iridescence is the strong
    color shift that makes the "Blue Gem" trade
    valuable.

  The .ts factory uses these via the existing PART 5
  `material: '<name>'` field — no syntax change. The
  same 8 names are also valid in JSON/JS
  `"material": "<name>"` strings.


45.5 — RENDERER-SIDE EXTENSIONS
-------------------------------

  The renderer's `buildMaterial` function (in index.html)
  extends with 4 new MeshPhysicalMaterial /
  MeshStandardMaterial properties. None of the existing
  PART 3 / PART 7 paths are touched.

  ── 45.5.1  iridescence / iridescenceIOR /
              iridescenceThicknessRange (NEW
              wire-through) ───────────────────────────────
    When ANY of the three is set on the object OR on
    the resolved base preset, the `wantsPhysical`
    switch flips to true (so the material is built as
    MeshPhysicalMaterial even if no other PBR
    extension was requested). The values are clamped
    to the safe ranges (0-1, 1.0-3.0, [50, 1200]
    respectively) and copied onto the material. The
    same priority rule from PART 3 / PART 7 applies:
    explicit per-object value > base preset value >
    default (1.0 / 0.0 / [100, 400]).

  ── 45.5.2  specularIntensity / specularColor (NEW
              wire-through) ─────────────────────────────────
    These are F0 knobs for dielectrics. They work on
    BOTH MeshStandardMaterial and MeshPhysicalMaterial,
    so they are NOT part of the `wantsPhysical` switch.
    The values are clamped to the safe range (0-1 for
    intensity; any color) and copied onto the
    material.

  ── 45.5.3  attenuationColor / attenuationDistance
              (NEW wire-through) ───────────────────────────
    These only matter when `transmission > 0` (the
    absorption color of a tinted glass / scope lens).
    The renderer wires them through on
    MeshPhysicalMaterial only; they're set on the
    material when the parent preset / object asks for
    them. Defaults: 0xffffff attenuationColor, 0.5
    attenuationDistance (matching Three.js's own
    defaults for MeshPhysicalMaterial).

  ── 45.5.4  The `wantsPhysical` switch (UPDATED, NOT
              CHANGED) ─────────────────────────────────────
    The existing switch already flips on for
    `clearcoat` / `sheen` / `anisotropy` /
    `transmission`. v1.22 / v8.14 adds `iridescence` /
    `iridescenceIOR` / `iridescenceThicknessRange` to
    the same condition. The behavior is unchanged for
    objects that don't set any of these: they still
    build as MeshStandardMaterial, exactly as before.
    The switch extension is a strict additive change
    (a new condition makes MORE objects use
    PhysicalMaterial, never fewer).


45.6 — .TS FACTORY / JSON BLUEPRINT CONTRACT
--------------------------------------------

  The .ts factory / JSON blueprint that wants a CS2-style
  subject follows this 4-step pattern:

    1.  Set `meta.cs2Finish` to one of the 9 finish
        strings (45.2.1–45.2.9). E.g. `cs2Finish:
        'anodizedblue'`, `cs2Finish: 'casehardened'`,
        `cs2Finish: 'damascussteel'`, `cs2Finish:
        'custompaintjob'`, `cs2Finish: 'patina'`,
        `cs2Finish: 'sprayed'`, etc. The factory MAY
        also pass `cs2Finish` to `cs2PbrProfile(finish,
        opts)` directly to get the property bundle for
        non-material contexts (e.g. authoring a UI that
        previews the finish).

    2.  (Optional) Set `meta.cs2Wear` to a number 0-1
        (e.g. `cs2Wear: 0.22` for a Field-Tested item)
        OR a string 'fn' / 'mw' / 'ft' / 'ww' / 'bs'
        (the renderer maps the string to the tier's
        midpoint: fn → 0.04, mw → 0.11, ft → 0.27, ww
        → 0.42, bs → 0.72). When the .ts factory wants
        a static single-wear model it sets this once
        and the renderer applies the wear to the
        material; when it wants a USER-SLIDABLE wear
        (e.g. an inspector panel slider) it sets
        `meta.cs2WearUserTunable: true` and the
        renderer publishes a `wearSlider` widget on
        `userData.sculptRuntime.wearSlider` (45.10.5).

    3.  (Optional) Set `meta.cs2WearRemap = {min, max}`
        to clamp the skin's float range (45.3.2). When
        absent, full 0.00–1.00.

    4.  (Optional) Set `meta.cs2WearMask = { size,
        opts }` to ask the renderer to build a
        procedural wear mask texture (45.3.5). The .ts
        factory / JSON blueprint may instead pass its
        own pre-built CanvasTexture via `meta.wearMap`
        (the .ts factory never loads an external image
        file — the no-external-asset rule from PART 5
        is unchanged).

  Worked example — a CS2 Karambit with Case Hardened
  finish at Field-Tested wear (TS factory):

    // meta block
    const meta = {
      style: 'low-poly',         // or 'mid-poly',
                                 // 'photoreal', etc.
      subject: 'karambit',
      cs2Finish: 'casehardened', // PART 45.2.6
      cs2Wear: 0.27,             // PART 45.3.1
                                 // (FT midpoint)
      cs2WearRemap: {min: 0.00, max: 1.00}, // no clamp
    };

    // In createKarambitModel():
    const profile = window.lblSpec.helpers.cs2PbrProfile(
      meta.cs2Finish
    );
    const bladeMat = new THREE.MeshPhysicalMaterial({
      color: 0x667788,           // Case Hardened base
      ...profile,                // {metalness:1.0,
                                 //  roughness:0.25,
                                 //  iridescence:0.5,
                                 //  iridescenceIOR:1.4,
                                 //  envMapIntensity:1.3}
    });
    window.lblSpec.helpers.applyWear(
      bladeMat,
      meta.cs2Wear,              // 0.27
      1.0,                       // baseMetal (full
                                 // metal substrate)
      0x667788                   // baseColor (for
                                 // desaturation)
    );
    // ... build the blade mesh with bladeMat ...

  The .ts factory doesn't write ANY of the PBR numbers
  inline; it pulls them from `cs2PbrProfile` and
  `applyWear`. This is the same "drive everything from
  a helper" rule as PART 39.5 + PART 44.8.4.


45.7 — JSON / JS PATH (mirror)
------------------------------

  The JSON/JS path uses the same two meta keys, exactly
  the same way. The renderer's existing `validateAndBuild()`
  (the JSON/JS entry point) reads `meta.cs2Finish` and
  `meta.cs2Wear` after the rest of the meta block is
  parsed, and applies the same `cs2PbrProfile` +
  `applyWear` helper pipeline before material
  instantiation.

  Example JSON fragment:

    {
      "meta": {
        "style": "mid-poly",
        "subject": "butterfly-knife",
        "cs2Finish": "anodizedmulticolored",
        "cs2Wear": "ft",
        "cs2WearRemap": { "min": 0.00, "max": 1.00 }
      },
      "objects": [
        {
          "id": "blade",
          "material": "anodizedmulticolored",
          "color": "#ff6600"
        }
      ]
    }

  The renderer resolves `meta.cs2Wear: "ft"` to 0.27
  (the FT midpoint), passes the anodizedmulticolored
  finish through `cs2PbrProfile`, and applies wear. The
  JSON blueprint's per-object `material:
  'anodizedmulticolored'` is the new MAT_DB preset name
  from 45.4 (a 1:1 alias to the finish's full property
  bundle).

  The TS path's `cs2WearUserTunable: true` flag is also
  honored on the JSON path: when set, the renderer
  publishes the wear slider on the inspector (45.10.5).
  This is the only way a JSON blueprint can request a
  user-tunable wear, since JSON has no per-frame
  tick() callback.


45.8 — WHEN TO OPT IN
---------------------

  The "extra capability" rule. PART 45 is a CAPABILITY,
  not a REQUIREMENT. The .ts factory / JSON blueprint
  sets `meta.cs2Finish` ONLY in the 5 use cases below.
  For every other subject, the .ts factory leaves
  `meta.cs2Finish` unset and the model loads on the
  default PART 3 + PART 41 + PART 44 pipeline.

  ── 45.8.1  Knives and blades ─────────────────────────────
    Karambit, butterfly knife, Bowie knife, M9 bayonet,
    bayonet, classic knife, skeleton knife, stiletto,
    survival knife, tactical knife, Ursus knife, nomad
    knife, shadow daggers, any CS:GO knife / CS2 knife.
    Plus fantasy blades (sword, dagger, machete) the
    user has asked for in a CS2-style finish. The most
    common finish choices: casehardened, damascussteel,
    anodizedblue, anodizedred, fade (anodized
    multicolored), Slaughter (custom paint job).

  ── 45.8.2  Guns and weapon hardware ──────────────────────
    AK-47, M4A4, M4A1-S, AWP, Desert Eagle, Glock-18,
    USP-S, P250, Five-SeveN, Tec-9, CZ75-Auto, revolver,
    any rifle / pistol / shotgun / SMG. Plus the weapon
    HARDWARE the user might request as a standalone
    (suppressor, scope, grip, stock, magazine). Common
    finish choices: anodizednavy, anodizedred, redline
    (gunsmith), the various snake / jungle / urban
    camos (hydrographic), asiimov (custom paint job),
    the dragon-tattoo / fire-serpent / howl finishes
    (custom paint job).

  ── 45.8.3  Polished / brushed / gunmetal metal props ────
    Any standalone prop the user has asked for in
    polished / brushed / dark steel / gunmetal.
    Examples: a sword with a polished blade + leather
    grip; a chrome motorcycle exhaust; a brushed-steel
    watch case; a Damascus steel ring; a polished steel
    letter opener; a dark steel hammer head. Common
    finish choices: polishedmetal, brushedsteel,
    gunmetal, damascussteel.

  ── 45.8.4  CS2 displays / showcases ─────────────────────
    Any subject the user is rendering for a CS2-themed
    display, a weapon showcase, a skin-trade preview,
    a Battle-Scarred hero render, a Fade gradient
    display, etc. The user almost always names a CS2
    finish here ("make me a Battle-Scarred AWP", "show
    me this Fade AK", "I want a Case Hardened display
    for this Blue Gem"). The .ts factory takes the
    named finish directly.

  ── 45.8.5  Explicit user requests ───────────────────────
    The user has used the words "CS2", "CS:GO", "skin",
    "finish", "anodized", "case hardened", "Doppler",
    "Fade", "Slaughter", "Asiimov", "Hyper Beast",
    "patina", "Damascus", "brushed", "polished steel",
    "gunmetal", "anodized [color]" or similar. When
    ANY of these tokens appear in the prompt, the .ts
    factory MAY set `cs2Finish` (the user asked for
    it). When the user hasn't used any of these tokens
    AND the subject isn't one of 45.8.1–45.8.4, the
    .ts factory should NOT set `cs2Finish`.

  Subjects where `cs2Finish` is almost NEVER appropriate
  (the .ts factory should leave it unset):
    - Fabric / clothing / character apparel (no metal
      substrate to anodize / patina / Damascus)
    - Skin / face / hair / character anatomy (organic,
      not metal)
    - Wood / paper / cardboard / natural materials
    - Plants / trees / flowers / food / organic shapes
    - Glass / crystal / liquid (use PART 3's existing
      `transmission` / `glass` preset, not CS2
      finishes)
    - Magic / fantasy / glow / emissive subjects (use
      PART 41 + PART 44's `magiccrystal` / `energy`
      preset, not CS2 finishes)
    - Vehicles (full cars, planes, ships — the WHOLE
      vehicle is too varied to be one CS2 finish; use
      the PART 3 + PART 44 default and let the user
      opt into a metal-finish for the BODY only)
    - Architecture (buildings, rooms, furniture)
    - Any subject where a metal finish would look
      wrong (a teddy bear shouldn't be "casehardened"
      — E30 would surface a warning if the user asks
      anyway)

  E30 (45.9) is the validator that catches these
  mismatches — it ONLY fires when `meta.cs2Finish` is
  set, so a model with NO CS2 opt-in never sees a
  warning.


45.9 — RENDERER-SIDE RUNTIME CONTRACT
-------------------------------------

  Adds 1 new non-blocking validator to the existing 29:

  ── 45.9.1  E30 — CS2 profile compliance (PART 45.2) ─────
    Fires ONLY when `root.userData.cs2Finish` (TS path)
    OR `meta.cs2Finish` (JSON/JS path) is set. Checks:

    a) The material the .ts factory / JSON blueprint
       actually built is consistent with the requested
       finish's expected substrate. A "casehardened"
       finish on a `metalness: 0.0` material is a
       mismatch (Case Hardened needs full metal).
    b) The material uses the right PBR property for
       the finish's defining feature. "anodizedblue"
       should set `clearcoat`; "casehardened" should
       set `iridescence`; "brushedsteel" should set
       `anisotropy`; "damascussteel" should set BOTH
       `iridescence` AND `anisotropy`.
    c) When `meta.cs2Wear` is set, the wear amount is
       inside the declared `meta.cs2WearRemap` range
       (if any).

    E30 is NON-BLOCKING — the model still loads
    regardless of any of these checks; a wrong
    combination surfaces a single warning toast + a
    red spec chip, exactly like E1-E29.

  ── 45.9.2  E30 is OPT-IN by construction ────────────────
    A model with NO `cs2Finish` and NO `cs2Wear` field
    never triggers E30 — the validator's first check is
    "is `cs2Finish` set?" and returns null (clean) when
    it isn't. This is what makes PART 45 a strict
    "extra capability" — the model's load behavior is
    IDENTICAL to a non-CS2 model.

  ── 45.9.3  E30 does NOT change load behavior ─────────────
    Same non-blocking pattern as E1-E29. The model
    always loads; the warning is informational.


45.10 — CANONICAL HELPERS (re-exported on window.lblSpec)
---------------------------------------------------------

  5 new helpers join the existing PART 35 + PART 39 +
  PART 40 + PART 41 + PART 42 + PART 43 + PART 44 bundle.
  The factory and the renderer's E30 validator always see
  the same behavior because they share the same
  implementations.

  ── 45.10.1  cs2PbrProfile(finish, opts) ─────────────────
    PART 45.2. Returns the property bundle for a given
    finish string. The bundle is a plain object the
    .ts factory spreads onto its material constructor:
        const profile = cs2PbrProfile('anodizedblue');
        // → { metalness: 1.0, roughness: 0.08,
        //     clearcoat: 0.4, clearcoatRoughness: 0.05,
        //     envMapIntensity: 1.3 }
    Plus the optional `opts.color` / `opts.wearAmount`
    overrides. The helper is the SINGLE SOURCE OF TRUTH
    for the 9 finish profiles in 45.2.1–45.2.9; the .ts
    factory never writes the numbers inline.

  ── 45.10.2  applyWear(mat, wearAmount, baseMetal,
                  baseColor) ─────────────────────────────
    PART 45.3.3. Mutates a MeshPhysicalMaterial in
    place: roughness ramps toward the global worn
    default (0.7), metalness ramps toward `baseMetal`
    (default 1.0), color desaturates 30% toward
    `baseColor * 0.7`. The helper detects "skip
    desaturation" finishes (Patina, Damascus) from
    `mat.userData.cs2Finish` and skips the color pass
    for those. Returns the same material for chaining.

  ── 45.10.3  cs2PbrProfile into materialVariants ────────
    The PART 41.6 `materialVariants` helper is EXTENDED
    with 1 new family: `cs2-finish`. The 6 existing
    families (skin + cloth + leather + metal + bone +
    magic) stay exactly as they were; the new family
    is a 7th entry the .ts factory can pull by name:
        const mat = materialVariants('cs2-finish',
          { finish: 'casehardened', wear: 0.27 }
        );
    Same shape as the existing 6 family pulls.

  ── 45.10.4  cs2WearMaskTexture(size, opts) ──────────────
    PART 45.3.5. Procedural wear mask generator.
    Returns a CanvasTexture whose red channel is the
    wear mask (0 at no-wear, 1 at full-wear). Default
    `opts` combine a low-frequency noise field (the
    "scattered blemishes" look) with an edge-detection
    mask (the "edge wear" look). The .ts factory can
    override either:
        const mask = cs2WearMaskTexture(256, {
          noiseScale: 8.0,    // bigger = smaller
                              //  blemishes
          edgeBias: 0.4,      // 0 = uniform, 1 = edge-only
          seed: 12345,
        });
    The factory sets `mat.wearMap = mask` and the
    renderer multiplies it against
    `mat.userData.wearAmount`. (The exact
    multiplication lives in `buildMaterial` — see
    45.5.5 / the renderer's wear-mask path.)

  ── 45.10.5  wearSlider (TS path only, OPTIONAL) ─────────
    When `meta.cs2WearUserTunable: true`, the renderer
    publishes a `wearSlider` widget on
    `root.userData.sculptRuntime.wearSlider`. The
    widget is a single-float input bound to the
    model's `cs2Wear` value. Each change re-invokes
    `applyWear(mat, newWear, ...)` so the user sees
    the wear slide in real time. The widget surfaces
    in the inspector's "Action Hooks" / "Materials"
    panel.


45.11 — WHAT PART 45 DOES NOT INTRODUCE
---------------------------------------

    - No new mandatory field on any existing userData
      namespace (the new fields in 45.5.1 `iridescence` /
      `iridescenceIOR` / `iridescenceThicknessRange`,
      45.5.2 `specularIntensity` / `specularColor`,
      45.5.3 `attenuationColor` / `attenuationDistance`,
      45.6.1 `cs2Finish`, 45.6.2 `cs2Wear`, 45.6.3
      `cs2WearRemap`, 45.6.4 `cs2WearMask`, 45.6.4
      `cs2WearUserTunable` are ALL OPTIONAL; the current
      renderer ignores unknown keys safely)
    - No new export path (the existing GLB / OBJ / STL /
      FBX / DAE / USDZ / .ts paths are unchanged)
    - No new dependency (iridescence and
      specularIntensity are core MeshPhysicalMaterial /
      MeshStandardMaterial properties that have existed
      in Three.js for years; the renderer just hadn't
      been wiring them through)
    - No new validator that blocks the model from
      loading (E30 is non-blocking, like E1-E29 before
      it)
    - No change to the existing PART 1-44 rules (PART
      45 layers on top, never replaces — see 45.0.1)
    - No change to the existing E1-E29 validators (they
      stay exactly as they were in v1.21 / v8.13)
    - No change to PART 38.22's style agnosticism (PART
      45 is also style-agnostic in scope; see 45.0.3)
    - No change to PART 38.14's GLB-first architecture
      (PART 45 extends it with the same viewer-adapter
      pattern; the .ts factory still SHIPS THE GLB and
      PART 45's opt-in is just an additional meta field)
    - No change to PART 39.5's factory + options pattern
      (PART 45 reinforces it — the .ts factory uses the
      same options + helper pattern from 45.6)
    - No change to PART 41.6's 4 default material
      families (PART 45.10.3 extends them with 1 new
      family; the existing 4+2 = 6 stay exactly as
      they were)
    - No change to PART 44.3's bone + magic families
      (the 6 family hierarchy from PART 44.3 stays
      exactly as it was; PART 45.10.3 adds a 7th)
    - No change to the project's "Low Poly 3D" name
    - No REMOVAL of any existing finish or material
      preset (the existing 30+ MAT_DB entries stay
      exactly as they were; the 8 new CS2 entries are
      PURE ADDITIONS)
    - No REMOVAL or WEAKENING of any existing
      iridescence / clearcoat / sheen / anisotropy /
      transmission path (the existing 4 extensions stay
      exactly as they were; the 4 new
      MeshPhysicalMaterial properties are PURE ADDITIONS
      to the same `wantsPhysical` switch)


45.12 — CROSS-REFERENCES
------------------------

  PART 45 layers on top of, never replaces:
    - PART 1  (factory contract)         — TS path
    - PART 3  (material contract)        — TS path
    - PART 3 advanced PBR (clearcoat/
      sheen/anisotropy/transmission)     — TS path (45.5)
    - PART 5  (texture rules)            — both paths
    - PART 7  (PBR extensions)           — JSON/JS path
    - PART 17 (image-to-3D analysis)     — TS image path
    - PART 27 (image-to-3D analysis)     — JSON/JS image
    - PART 39 (3D modeling techniques)   — both paths
    - PART 39.5 (factory + options)      — 45.6
    - PART 41 (high-detail generation)   — both paths
    - PART 41.6 (materialVariants)       — 45.10.3
    - PART 42 (character production)     — both paths
    - PART 43 (refinement + style lock)  — both paths
    - PART 44 (professional pipeline)    — both paths
    - PART 44.3 (6-family material)      — 45.10.3
    - PART 44.7 (TS viewer adapter)      — 45.6
    - PART 38.22 (style agnosticism)     — 45.0.3
    - PART 38.14 (GLB-first arch)        — 45.11

  The maintainer's "3D Technical Reference — CS2 to Three.js
  Mapping" research notebook (id `30ec3dba-5a20-4980-8886-
  ce89b506b634`, July 2026) is the source doc. The notebook
  catalogues 9 finish styles (8 CS2 + 1 bonus Damascus), 8
  material preset recipes, the CS2 wear system with 5 tiers,
  the per-skin float-clipping rules, the spatial wear
  glossary, and the Three.js implementation notes. PART 45
  layers those onto the LBL spec's PART 1-44 stack as an
  opt-in capability for the .ts factory and JSON/JS
  blueprint authors.

45.13 — COMPONENT RECIPE DATABASE (WEAPONS, BLADES, METAL)
---------------------------------------------------------

  The 5th section of the maintainer's CS2 notebook is a
  reference table of WHICH Three.js primitive + WHICH
  proportions + WHICH PBR setting to use for the
  component parts of a CS2-style weapon. This section
  preserves that reference as a CONTENT GUIDE for the
  .ts factory / JSON blueprint that wants to author a
  weapon subject (per the 45.8.1 / 45.8.2 / 45.8.3 use
  cases). It does NOT introduce new rules or new
  helpers — it curates the existing PART 2 (primitives)
  + PART 3 (materials) + PART 39 (3D modeling
  techniques) + PART 41 (high-detail generation) +
  PART 42 (character production) + PART 45 (CS2 PBR
  profile) PARTs into a per-component cheat sheet.

  ── 45.13.1  Knife components ─────────────────────────

    Blade types:
    +---------------+--------------------+---------------+----------+----------+
    | Type          | Three.js geometry  | W:L ratio     | PBR      | Notes    |
    +---------------+--------------------+---------------+----------+----------+
    | Clip-point    | ExtrudeGeometry    | 1:3.5         | 1.0/0.15 | Notch at |
    |               |                    |               |          | tip      |
    | Drop-point    | ExtrudeGeometry    | 1:3           | 0.9/0.2  | Smooth   |
    |               |                    |               |          | spine    |
    |               |                    |               |          | curve    |
    | Tanto         | ExtrudeGeometry    | 1:3           | 1.0/0.1  | Angular  |
    |               |                    |               |          | chisel   |
    |               |                    |               |          | tip      |
    | Karambit      | ExtrudeGeometry    | 1:2 (curved)  | 1.0/0.05 | Extreme  |
    |               |                    |               |          | curve    |
    | Falchion      | LatheGeometry /    | 1:4           | 1.0/0.15 | Wide,    |
    |               | Extrude            |               |          | slight   |
    |               |                    |               |          | curve    |
    | Gut Hook      | Extrude + CSG      | 1:2.5         | 1.0/0.3  | Hook via |
    |               | Subtract           |               |          | boolean  |
    +---------------+--------------------+---------------+----------+----------+

    Handle construction:
    +---------+----------------+-----------+-----------+
    | Part    | Geometry       | PBR       | Notes     |
    +---------+----------------+-----------+-----------+
    | Tang    | BoxGeometry    | 1.0/0.4   | Structural|
    | Frame   |                |           | core      |
    | Scales  | ExtrudeGeometry| 0.0/0.75  | G10 /     |
    | (×2)    |                |           | Micarta / |
    |         |                |           | Wood      |
    | Screws  | Cylinder-      | 1.0/0.35  | Phillips  |
    | (×4)    | Geometry       |           | head      |
    | Guard   | BoxGeometry    | 1.0/0.3   | Cross     |
    |         |                |           | guard or  |
    |         |                |           | none      |
    | Pommel  | Cylinder-      | 1.0/0.35  | End cap   |
    |         | Geometry       |           |           |
    | Lanyard | TorusGeometry  | 0.0/0.9   | Paracord  |
    |         |                |           | loop      |
    +---------+----------------+-----------+-----------+

  ── 45.13.2  Pistol components ────────────────────────

    +---------------+--------------------+--------------------+
    | Part          | Geometry           | Boolean ops        |
    +---------------+--------------------+--------------------+
    | Slide         | BoxGeometry        | Subtract: ejection |
    |               |                    | port, rail         |
    | Frame         | ExtrudeGeometry +  | Union: grip;       |
    |               | Box                | Subtract: mag well |
    | Barrel        | CylinderGeometry   | Subtract: inner    |
    |               |                    | bore               |
    | Trigger Guard | ExtrudeGeometry    | Union: to frame    |
    | Magazine      | BoxGeometry        | Subtract: feed     |
    |               |                    | lips               |
    | Sights (×2)   | BoxGeometry        | Union: to slide    |
    | Grip Texture  | Normal map         | NOT geometry       |
    +---------------+--------------------+--------------------+

  ── 45.13.3  Rifle components (AK-47, M4, AWP) ───────

    +-----------------+----------------------+
    | Part            | Geometry             |
    +-----------------+----------------------+
    | Receiver (Upper)| BoxGeometry          |
    | Receiver (Lower)| BoxGeometry          |
    | Barrel          | CylinderGeometry     |
    | Handguard       | BoxGeometry /        |
    |                 | CylinderGeometry     |
    | Stock           | BoxGeometry          |
    | Magazine        | BoxGeometry          |
    | Pistol Grip     | ExtrudeGeometry      |
    | Scope (AWP)     | TubeGeometry +       |
    |                 | SphereGeometry       |
    | Muzzle Device   | CylinderGeometry     |
    | Bolt Carrier    | BoxGeometry          |
    +-----------------+----------------------+

  ── 45.13.4  Boolean operations guide ────────────────

    +-----------+-----------------+------------------+
    | Operation | Library         | Use case         |
    +-----------+-----------------+------------------+
    | Subtract  | three-bvh-csg   | Holes, cutouts,  |
    |           |                 | slots, ejection  |
    |           |                 | ports            |
    | Union     | three-bvh-csg   | Merging          |
    |           |                 | components (frame|
    |           |                 | + grip)          |
    | Intersect | three-bvh-csg   | Complex shape    |
    |           |                 | intersections    |
    +-----------+-----------------+------------------+

  ── 45.13.5  Edge treatment ─────────────────────────

    +----------------+----------+--------------------------+
    | Zone           | Treatment| How                      |
    +----------------+----------+--------------------------+
    | Cutting edge   | Hard     | computeVertexNormals()   |
    |                | crease   | + manual normal          |
    |                |          | adjustment               |
    | Spine-to-plate | Hard edge| Split vertices at seam   |
    | Sawback teeth  | All hard | No smoothing groups      |
    | Guard corners  | Hard     | BoxGeometry naturally    |
    |                |          | hard                     |
    | Handle scales  | Smooth   | Subdivision or smooth    |
    |                |          | normals                  |
    | Grip checkering| Normal   | NOT geometry             |
    |                | map      |                          |
    +----------------+----------+--------------------------+

    45.13.6  The 45.13 component database is a
            CONTENT GUIDE for the JS blueprint (same
            as the JSON path). The blueprint author
            references 45.13 to pick the right
            `objects[].geometry` shape + PBR material.
            The cheat sheet is purely a reference, not
            a runtime contract.


45.14 — VOCABULARY GLOSSARY (EN / VI)
-------------------------------------

  The 6th section of the maintainer's CS2 notebook is a
  bilingual (EN / Vietnamese) glossary of the technical
  terms the AI needs to recognize across prompt + image
  + JSON/JS inputs. PART 45.14 preserves the EN/VI
  side-by-side pairing for the 5 most-misused terms in
  the weapon / metal-finish subject area. This is a
  REFERENCE resource, not a runtime contract — the .ts
  factory / JSON blueprint doesn't read this section;
  the AI uses it to correctly identify subject matter
  in the user's prompt or reference image.

  ── 45.14.1  Core industry terms (Thuật Ngữ Cốt Lõi) ─

    +----------------------+----------------------+--------------------------------+
    | EN Term              | VI Term              | Definition & 3D application    |
    +----------------------+----------------------+--------------------------------+
    | Surface              | Khuyết điểm bề mặt  | Microscopic surface           |
    | Imperfections        |                      | variations that break up      |
    |                      |                      | uniform specular reflections. |
    |                      |                      | Essential for photorealism.   |
    |                      |                      | Examples: fingerprints on     |
    |                      |                      | glass, dust layers on         |
    |                      |                      | screens, micro-scratches on   |
    |                      |                      | polished metal.               |
    +----------------------+----------------------+--------------------------------+
    | Weathering / Aging   | Phong hóa / Lão hóa  | Surface degradation caused by |
    |                      |                      | environmental exposure (rain, |
    |                      |                      | temperature, sunlight) over  |
    |                      |                      | time. Examples: oxidation on  |
    |                      |                      | metals (rust / patina),      |
    |                      |                      | sun-fading on paint, moss    |
    |                      |                      | growth on damp grips.        |
    +----------------------+----------------------+--------------------------------+
    | Wear and Tear        | Hao mòn sử dụng      | Physical damage from human    |
    |                      |                      | handling or operational       |
    |                      |                      | friction. Examples: blunted  |
    |                      |                      | knife tips, hand-worn glossy  |
    |                      |                      | grips, paint peeling on      |
    |                      |                      | high-friction weapon parts.  |
    +----------------------+----------------------+--------------------------------+
    | Edge Wear            | Mòn cạnh viền        | Damage concentrated along the |
    |                      |                      | sharp boundaries, corners, or |
    |                      |                      | protrusions of a model.       |
    |                      |                      | Examples: paint scraping off |
    |                      |                      | along the slide edges of a   |
    |                      |                      | Glock or the guard corners   |
    |                      |                      | of a Bowie knife.            |
    +----------------------+----------------------+--------------------------------+
    | Procedural Grunge    | Cáu bẩn thuật toán   | Procedurally generated grime |
    |                      |                      | or dirt, often mask-blended   |
    |                      |                      | using Ambient Occlusion (AO) |
    |                      |                      | or curvature. Examples: dust |
    |                      |                      | settling in recessed grooves,|
    |                      |                      | gun grease building up in    |
    |                      |                      | hard-to-clean weapon         |
    |                      |                      | crevices.                    |
    +----------------------+----------------------+--------------------------------+

  ── 45.14.2  Spatial modifiers (5 EN / VI terms) ─────

    +----------------------+----------------------+
    | EN Term              | VI Term              |
    +----------------------+----------------------+
    | Tip-to-spine         | Gradient từ mũi đến  |
    | gradient             | sống dao             |
    | Edge-heavy wear      | Mòn tập trung ở      |
    |                      | cạnh                 |
    | Friction-point wear  | Mòn điểm ma sát      |
    | Speckled / Spotted   | Dạng đốm             |
    | Exposed core         | Lộ lõi               |
    +----------------------+----------------------+

    EN definitions:
      • Tip-to-spine gradient: damage concentration
        decreases gradually from the tip towards the
        mid-body.
      • Edge-heavy wear: scratches or paint loss
        occurring exclusively along high-exposure outer
        edges.
      • Friction-point wear: concentrated wear at
        mechanical contact points (magazine slides,
        knife locks).
      • Speckled / Spotted: scattered, point-like
        occurrences of rust or paint chipping.
      • Exposed core: complete removal of an outer
        layer, showing the raw underlying material.

  ── 45.14.3  Damage types × PBR map impact (the
              4-way rubric from the notebook) ────────

    +--------------+----------------+-----------------+---------------+----------------------+
    | Damage type  | Albedo impact   | Roughness map   | Metalness map | Normal/height map    |
    |              |                | impact          | impact        | impact               |
    +--------------+----------------+-----------------+---------------+----------------------+
    | Oxidation /  | Shifts to      | Increases       | Drops to 0.0  | Adds high-freq noise,|
    | Rust /       | orange-brown   | significantly   | (conductive   | pitting, and scaling |
    | Patina       | (rust) or      | (0.8 - 1.0)     | metal turns   | surface volume.      |
    |              | dark-green     | due to dry,     | into non-     |                      |
    |              | (patina).      | rough           | conductive    |                      |
    |              |                | microfacets.    | oxide).       |                      |
    +--------------+----------------+-----------------+---------------+----------------------+
    | Scratches /  | Exposes base   | Decreases at    | Increases to  | Creates sharp,      |
    | Dents        | metal          | scratch center  | 1.0 at        | linear indentations |
    |              | underneath     | (reveals shiny  | scratch       | or local coordinate |
    |              | (usually light | substrate);     | center if     | normals.             |
    |              | grey / silver).| increases at    | painted       |                      |
    |              |                | scratch lip.    | coating is    |                      |
    |              |                |                 | scraped off.  |                      |
    +--------------+----------------+-----------------+---------------+----------------------+
    | Smudges /    | Negligible     | Localized       | Unchanged.    | Unchanged.           |
    | Fingerprints | change.        | increases       |               |                      |
    |              |                | (0.4 - 0.6)     |               |                      |
    |              |                | where natural   |               |                      |
    |              |                | body oils       |               |                      |
    |              |                | distort         |               |                      |
    |              |                | reflections.    |               |                      |
    +--------------+----------------+-----------------+---------------+----------------------+
    | Dust / Dirt /| Matches        | Becomes         | Drops to 0.0  | Smooths out         |
    | Mud          | environmental  | completely      | (non-metallic | micro-structures    |
    |              | soil colors    | rough and       | soil          | (dust) or adds      |
    |              | (tan, brown,   | diffuse         | coating).     | thick volumes       |
    |              | grey).         | (0.9 - 1.0).    |               | (caked mud).        |
    +--------------+----------------+-----------------+---------------+----------------------+

    45.14.4  The vocabulary glossary is a REFERENCE
            RESOURCE for the JS blueprint author (same
            as the JSON path). The blueprint doesn't
            read it at runtime; the AI uses it to
            correctly identify subject matter in the
            user's prompt. The bilingual EN/VI
            pairing helps Vietnamese-speaking users
            find the right English term for the same
            concept.


45.15 — UPDATED CROSS-REFERENCES
--------------------------------

  PART 45 layers on top of, never replaces:
    - PART 1  (factory contract)         — TS path
    - PART 2  (primitives)               — TS path (45.13)
    - PART 3  (material contract)        — TS path
    - PART 3 advanced PBR (clearcoat/
      sheen/anisotropy/transmission)     — TS path (45.5)
    - PART 5  (texture rules)            — both paths
    - PART 7  (PBR extensions)           — JSON/JS path
    - PART 10 (CSG booleans)             — TS path (45.13.4)
    - PART 17 (image-to-3D analysis)     — TS image path
    - PART 27 (image-to-3D analysis)     — JSON/JS image
    - PART 39 (3D modeling techniques)   — both paths (45.13)
    - PART 39.5 (factory + options)      — 45.6
    - PART 41 (high-detail generation)   — both paths (45.13)
    - PART 41.6 (materialVariants)       — 45.10.3
    - PART 42 (character production)     — both paths (45.13)
    - PART 43 (refinement + style lock)  — both paths
    - PART 44 (professional pipeline)    — both paths
    - PART 44.3 (6-family material)      — 45.10.3
    - PART 44.7 (TS viewer adapter)      — 45.6
    - PART 38.22 (style agnosticism)     — 45.0.3
    - PART 38.14 (GLB-first arch)        — 45.11

  The maintainer's "3D Technical Reference — CS2 to Three.js
  Mapping" research notebook (id `30ec3dba-5a20-4980-8886-
  ce89b506b634`, July 2026) is the source doc. The notebook
  catalogues 9 finish styles (8 CS2 + 1 bonus Damascus), 8
  material preset recipes, the CS2 wear system with 5 tiers,
  the per-skin float-clipping rules, the spatial wear
  glossary (preserved in 45.14), the weapon component recipe
  database (knife / pistol / rifle — preserved in 45.13),
  the damage-type × PBR-map rubric (preserved in 45.14.3),
  and the Three.js implementation notes. PART 45
END OF PART 45 — v1.22 / v8.14 ADDITIONS: CS2 PBR MATERIAL PROFILE (OPT-IN)
Source: "3D Technical Reference — CS2 to Three.js Mapping" notebook
(id `30ec3dba-5a20-4980-8886-ce89b506b634`, July 2026). 9 finish
profiles (Anodized, Anodized Multicolored, Custom Paint Job, Gunsmith,
Hydrographic, Patina, Solid Color, Sprayed, Damascus Steel — the
last is the bonus finish the notebook treats separately from Case
Hardened patina) resolved via the new `cs2PbrProfile(finish, opts)`
helper. 8 new MAT_DB presets (`brushedsteel`, `gunmetal`,
`damascussteel`, `anodizedblue`, `anodizedred`, `anodizedgold`,
`casehardened`, plus the `polishedmetal` alias to the existing v6.4
`chrome`). CS2 wear system (5 tiers FN/MW/FT/WW/BS) bridged via
the new `applyWear(mat, wearAmount, baseMetal, baseColor)` helper
that mutates roughness / metalness / color in place. 4 new
MeshPhysicalMaterial properties wired through `buildMaterial`
(`iridescence` / `iridescenceIOR` / `iridescenceThicknessRange` /
`specularIntensity` / `specularColor` / `attenuationColor` /
`attenuationDistance` — the first 3 also extend the
`wantsPhysical` switch). 1 new non-blocking validator E30
(CS2 profile compliance — fires ONLY when `cs2Finish` is set,
so a non-CS2 model is unchanged). 5 new canonical helpers
(`cs2PbrProfile`, `applyWear`, the `cs2-finish` materialVariants
family, `cs2WearMaskTexture`, `wearSlider`) re-exported on
`window.lblSpec.helpers` alongside the PART 35 + PART 39 + PART
40 + PART 41 + PART 42 + PART 43 + PART 44 bundle. The
`materialVariants` helper from PART 41.6 is EXTENDED with 1
new family (cs2-finish); the existing 4+2 = 6 families stay
exactly as they were. PART 45 is an EXTRA CAPABILITY for
weapons, blades, and metal-finish subjects (see 45.8 for the
"when to opt in" rule); it is NOT a rule every model must
follow. Style-agnostic in scope (45.0.3) but content-specific
in subject (45.0.2 + 45.8). No new dependency (iridescence and
specularIntensity are core Three.js material properties that
have existed for years; the renderer just hadn't been wiring
them through). No new export path, no new mandatory field,
no removed / weakened rule, no removed / weakened existing
extension, no removed / weakened existing MAT_DB preset. No
change to PART 38.22's style agnosticism, no change to PART
38.14's GLB-first architecture, no change to PART 39.5's
factory + options pattern, no change to PART 41.6's existing
4 default families, no change to PART 44.3's existing bone +
magic families. Strict upgrade, zero downgrades.

================================================================================
PART 67 — v1.23 / v8.15 ADDITIONS: OPT-IN CAPABILITY BUNDLE
================================================================================

  PART 67 layers onto PARTs 1-45 as an OPT-IN capability
  bundle for the Prompt To Js path. The
  full spec lives in Prompt_To_Ts.txt (sections 67.1 -
  67.11) and Prompt_To_Json.txt (section 67.1 - 67.2 +
  worked example). This file inherits the same opt-in
  rules: PART 67 is an EXTRA CAPABILITY, not a rule; a
  model that doesn't use any of the new meta.* fields
  loads identically to v1.22 / v8.14.

  SUMMARY (apply the same rules on this path):
    - 7 cross-primitive shape generators
      (makeDisc / makeRing / makeArc / makeHemisphere /
      makeRibbon / makeCrossPlanes / makeHelix) — opt-in
      via meta.geometryPipeline or per-part primitive
      string.
    - 6 deform modifiers (taper / twist / bend / spherize
      / inflate / applyNoise) dispatched by
      applyModifierStack — opt-in via
      meta.geometryPipeline.modifiers.
    - 1 vertex-channel helper (fillVertexChannel) — opt-in
      via meta.vertexData.
    - 1 UV projection helper (projectUV) — opt-in via
      meta.uv.projection (now accepts 7 values: shapeDefault
      / planar / radial / cylindrical / spherical / box /
      alongLength).
    - 1 converge helper (convergeFaces) — opt-in via
      meta.geometryPipeline.converge.
    - 1 recolor helper (recolorByPalette) — opt-in via
      meta.colorPalette + material.userData.role.
    - 1 chain placement (placeChain) — opt-in via per-part
      declaration.
    - 1 time-driven material animation
      (applyMaterialAnimation) — opt-in via
      meta.materialAnimation.
    - 1 preflight check (preflightCheck) — opt-in via
      meta.usedPreflight.
    - 1 shadow profile (PART 44.5) — opt-in via
      meta.shadowProfile.
    - 6 new opt-in validators E31-E36 (modifier stack
      order, UV projection sanity, modifier stack
      safety, converge direction, recolor roles,
      preflight used). All fire ONLY when the
      corresponding opt-in flag is set, so a model
      that doesn't use PART 67 features is unchanged.

  Especially useful for weapons, blades, and
  metal-finish subjects (see Prompt_To_Ts.txt 67.0.4
  for the explicit use-case lists). Style-agnostic in
  scope but content-specific in subject. No new
  dependency, no new export path, no new mandatory
  field, no removed / weakened rule, no removed /
  weakened existing extension. Strict upgrade, zero
  downgrades.
================================================================================


================================================================================
PART 68-71 — v1.23 / v8.15 ADDITIONS: GEOMETRY + VFX + LOOKDEV
+ RANDOM + ALIGNMENT OPT-IN CAPABILITY BUNDLE
(from jasonsturges/three-low-poly + achrefelouafi/
LinearAbilityExtThreeJS)
================================================================================

  PART 68-71 layers onto PARTs 1-67 as a STRICTLY ADDITIVE
  capability bundle. The source is the maintainer's "two-repo
  analysis" working notebook (compiled 2026-09-05), which
  catalogues the architecture-level patterns from two external
  reference repos and translates them into the existing
  `lblSpec` / Three.js contract.

  CRITICAL RULES:
    - PART 68-71 is an EXTRA CAPABILITY, not a rule.
    - A model that doesn't use any of the new meta.* fields
      loads identically to v1.22 / v8.14.
    - All 50+ new helpers are OPT-IN by construction.
    - All 4 new validators (E37-E40) are non-blocking and
      fire ONLY when the corresponding opt-in flag is set.
    - Style-agnostic in scope, but especially useful for
      weapons, blades, and metal-finish subjects.

  SUMMARY (apply the same rules on this path):

  PART 68.1 — System helpers (P0)
    frame, lblFrameTick, mulberry32, splitmix32, deriveSubSeed,
    createRandom, makeObjectPool, makeAssetLoader

  PART 68.2 — 13 BufferGeometry classes (P1)
    StarGeometry, PolygonGeometry, AnnulusGeometry, HeartGeometry,
    SpadeGeometry, DiamondGeometry, BurstGeometry,
    ArchedSlabGeometry, ArchGeometry, EdgedBoxGeometry,
    MoldingGeometry, PumpkinGeometry, LeafGeometry

  PART 68.3 — Procedural textures (P1)
    makeCheckerboard, makeRadialGradient, makeLinearGradient
    + 5 new MAT_DB patterns (pat_checker_wood, pat_checker_stone,
    pat_radial_halo, pat_radial_aoe, pat_linear_heathaze)
    + 6 new MAT_DB color ramps (mat_ramp_fire, mat_ramp_ice,
    mat_ramp_poison, mat_ramp_holy, mat_ramp_shadow, mat_ramp_solar)

  PART 68.4 — Alignment helpers (P2)
    alignToEdge, alignToRow, alignToSurface, center

  PART 68.5 — Lifetime color ramps (P1)
    buildMatRamp(rampKeyOrObject) — pairs with `meta.matRamp`

  PART 68.6 — Sky helpers (P2)
    skyFullMoon, skyStarField

  PART 68.7 — Atmospheric effects (P1)
    atmosphereRain, atmosphereGroundFog, atmosphereDustMotes

  PART 68.8 — Lookdev helpers (P2)
    cyclorama, groundGrid, contactShadows

  PART 68.9 — Closed-form curves (P2)
    constantCurvatureArc, circularArc, helixPath, spiralPath

  PART 68.10 — Easing / Falloff (P2)
    easing (24 functions), falloff (8 functions)

  PART 68.11 — Bone segments (P2)
    boneSegments (20-bone default table for bone-anchored VFX)

  NEW META.* FIELDS (all opt-in):
    meta.matRamp?:  { birth, early, late, death } OR shorthand
                    string ('mat_ramp_fire' | 'mat_ramp_ice' | ...)
    meta.gizmo?:    { shape, range, minRange?, zoneRadius?,
                      gateWidth?, gateHeight?, ringRadius?, ringHover? }
    meta.vfx?:      { embers?, sparks?, motes?, debris?,
                      boneAnchored?, boneSegments? }
    meta.usesMathRandom?: boolean (E37 inspects this)

  NEW VALIDATORS (4 new, all non-blocking + opt-in):
    E37 — Sub-seed usage (fires when meta.usesMathRandom=true)
    E38 — Lifetime color ramp validity (fires when meta.matRamp set)
    E39 — Gizmo shape range (fires when meta.gizmo set)
    E40 — Bone segment coverage (fires when meta.vfx.boneAnchored=true)

  TOTAL: 50+ new `lblSpec.helpers` entries + 4 new validators.

  No new dependency, no new export path, no new mandatory field,
  no removed / weakened rule, no removed / weakened existing
  extension. Strict upgrade, zero downgrades.

  Especially useful for weapons, blades, and metal-finish
  subjects — see CHANGES_SUMMARY.md for the worked example
  (Karambit knife that uses mat_ramp_fire for the blade glow
  + bone segments for hand-anchored ember particles).
